Related: research, NASA, space exploration, ESA, astrophysics, space Tim Barnwell is a sub-editor and reporter at Silicon Republic
Silicon Republic
Opinion: Tech companies need to view content as infrastructure
2026-09-04

Opinion: Tech companies need to view content as infrastructure

Silicon Republic | 2026-09-04

Visit Original Website
Nahla Davies discusses where tech companies are going wrong when it comes to content strategies and how that affects the wider organisation. There’s a scene that plays out inside tech companies so often it barely registers anymore. A brief lands, a deadline tightens, a blogpost gets commissioned and everyone acts like the job’s done once something goes live. Then leadership looks at the numbers a few months later and starts asking why organic growth still feels weak, why the brand sounds interchangeable, and why all that publishing hasn’t translated into real authority. That question usually gets answered with the wrong diagnosis. Teams blame tone, timing, distribution, writers, search updates, even audience attention spans. What gets missed is the deeper issue. Content is still being treated like a campaign output when it’s doing the work of infrastructure. It shapes how a company gets discovered, understood, remembered and trusted. When that layer is weak, everything built on top of it starts wobbling. A lot of tech companies still don’t even consider information security relevant to content. Product wants a launch piece, sales wants something bottom funnel and SEO wants a cluster. At the same time, leadership wants thought leadership because a competitor has suddenly started sounding more confident in public. Content teams absorb all of it, turn the requests into assets, and keep the calendar moving. From the outside, that can look productive. The company is publishing, social posts are going out, newsletters have something to point at, and stakeholders can say they’re supporting content. Underneath, though, the operation is usually fragmented. Topics overlap, narratives shift, quality varies from brief to brief, and nobody’s really building a durable body of work. The company’s publishing history starts to look more like a pile of reactions than a coherent editorial system. That’s where the real cost shows up. It’s not only in underperforming articles or traffic that never compounds. It’s in the constant reinvention. Writers keep explaining the same core ideas from scratch. Editors keep fixing structural problems that should’ve been solved upstream. Subject matter experts keep repeating themselves in interviews because there’s no shared knowledge framework to build on. Everyone’s working, but very little of that work is strengthening the next piece. Tech leaders would never accept that model in engineering. They wouldn’t build core systems as disconnected one-offs and hope repetition somehow creates scale. Yet that’s exactly how content gets managed in plenty of organisations that claim to care about authority and long-term growth. The contradiction is right there in plain sight. Companies want the outcomes of infrastructure while funding the habits of improvisation. The strongest content teams rarely look glamorous from the inside. They’re not surviving on adrenaline and big campaign reveals. Instead, they treat content from the prism of B2B SEO, They’re usually the ones doing the quieter work that most companies postpone for too long. They have a defined editorial thesis, clear standards, documented workflows, refresh cycles, taxonomy and someone willing to see through the development of the whole system when every stakeholder wants a special exception. That kind of structure changes the function of every asset. An article stops being just something to fill a slot on the publishing calendar. It becomes part of a network. It has a role, a relationship to adjacent topics, a clear audience job, and a shelf life that’s actually being considered. Once that happens, content starts compounding. A new piece strengthens older ones, older ones support newer ones, and the whole library becomes more useful than the sum of its parts. Governance matters here more than most companies want to admit, not just when it comes to AI. It sounds dull, and dull things are hard to sell in rooms obsessed with velocity. Still, governance is usually the dividing line between teams that publish and teams that build. Someone needs to decide what quality means, what gets updated, what gets merged, what gets retired, what’s off strategy, and where the company’s point of view needs to stay consistent. Without that, volume just creates clutter faster. Most weak content systems don’t fail at the writing stage. They fail much earlier, usually in planning, ownership and decision-making. The brief arrives thin, the angle is vague, the audience is assumed rather than defined, and success gets reduced to some loose hope that the piece will rank, resonate, convert, or all three at once. Then the final draft gets judged as if the writer alone was responsible for the outcome. Teams that treat content as infrastructure work differently from the start. They know which subjects belong to them and which don’t. They know where the company has real expertise and where it’s simply adding to the noise. They involve content early enough that it can shape the narrative, not just package it. That’s a major difference. Once content is brought in only at the execution stage, it’s already been positioned as a formatting function instead of a strategic one. There’s also a financial misunderstanding baked into the old model. One-off content always looks cheaper because each asset is judged in isolation. Infrastructure thinking forces a different lens. A well-built article can educate prospects, strengthen internal messaging, support search visibility, feed sales enablement and sharpen brand perception at the same time. Its value doesn’t sit in a single campaign window. It keeps paying back because it was designed to live inside a system, not outside one. That’s why so many companies end up spending heavily on content and still feel underwhelmed by the results. They believe AI will have the same effect as in cybersecurity, but for now, they’re paying for deliverables, not durability. Then they act surprised when nothing seems to stick. Content only compounds when the organisation gives it the conditions to do so. Otherwise, it behaves exactly like any other short-term asset. It ships, it spikes, and it fades. Tech companies love talking about scale, systems, and long-term value creation. Content is where a lot of them quietly stop applying that logic. They still want fast output, immediate proof and endless flexibility, then wonder why the brand never develops real weight in the market. The companies that get further usually aren’t the ones publishing the most. They’re the ones building editorial structure that holds under pressure and improves over time. That’s where authority comes from. Once content gets treated as infrastructure, everything around it starts working harder. Search gets stronger, messaging gets clearer, expertise gets easier to trust and publishing finally stops pretending to be a strategy on its own. Nahla Davies is a software developer and tech writer. Before devoting her work full time to technical writing, she managed – among other intriguing things – to serve as a lead programmer at an Inc 5,000 experiential branding organisation, where clients include Samsung, Time Warner, Netflix and Sony. Don’t miss out on the knowledge you need to succeed. Sign up for the Daily Brief, Silicon Republic’s digest of need-to-know sci-tech news. OpenAI launches GPT-6 Astra
2026-09-04

OpenAI launches GPT-6 Astra

Silicon Republic | 2026-09-04

Visit Original Website
Company president Greg Brockman told media publications in a briefing that Astra’s launch could perhaps be viewed as the beginning of an era of artificial general intelligence. OpenAI’s GPT-6 Astra, the company’s newest AI model, is now available to a limited number of users after its unveiling yesterday (3 September). The company said it will become available “over the coming days … to all ChatGPT Plus, Pro, Business and Enterprise users, as well as through the OpenAI API, Microsoft Azure and AWS Bedrock”. The company said Astra is “the most capable model we have ever broadly deployed” and its first model to reach the ‘critical’ level of cybersecurity capability under its preparedness framework. Astra can, with the right tools and access, find previously unknown security flaws and develop new ways to exploit them across many well-protected systems without a person guiding each step, according to its maker, which said it therefore “significantly strengthened” protections against the new model taking harmful cyber actions through misuse or misalignment. The company added that the new model is “better-aligned” for “respecting safety and security boundaries and staying within its authorised scope” but less monitorable in tested “adversarial settings” than its predecessor, GPT‑5.6 Sol. Acknowledging the recent hack of Hugging Face by some of its other models during a testing scenario, OpenAI said it had built a new evaluation informed by that incident to evaluate “whether a model facing a difficult or impossible task will go beyond its intended scope”, and that “compared to GPT‑5.6 Sol, which without production safeguards went beyond the authorised target 48pc of the time, GPT‑6 Astra did this in 0pc of cases”. The company said Astra is “state-of-the-art on computer use, browsing, software engineering, cybersecurity, science and professional work”, and that it can be used to “take care of tedious tasks”, conduct online research or analyse scientific data, and “autonomously install and test software, and troubleshoot problems”. It is also described by its maker as “the best model for software engineering to date” and “a major advance for scientific discovery, mathematics and health”. OpenAI president Greg Brockman told media publications in a briefing that Astra’s launch could perhaps be viewed as the beginning of an era of artificial general intelligence. Alongside Astra, OpenAI yesterday also unveiled a new $1bn global initiative to “help frontline defenders use frontier AI cyber capabilities to protect essential services in the US and around the world”. ‘Daybreak for Frontline Defenders’ will aim “to help frontline defenders protect essential services” by bolstering and supporting their training in and deployment of AI, the company said. Earlier this week, OpenAI’s direct rival Anthropic launched the latest iterations of Claude Fable and Claude Mythos, which it described as “the world’s most advanced models for coding and knowledge work”. DeCare’s Maureen Walsh succeeds Colin Hunt as Ibec president
2026-09-04

DeCare’s Maureen Walsh succeeds Colin Hunt as Ibec president

Silicon Republic | 2026-09-04

Visit Original Website
Walsh said it is a privilege to represent Ireland’s businesses during a time of considerable change for the economy. Ibec, Ireland’s representative body for business and employers, has appointed Maureen Walsh as its president until 2027. She was elected by the organisation’s national council and will succeed Dr Colin Hunt, it was announced yesterday (3 September). Walsh is the managing director of DeCare Dental Insurance, which she has led from the start-up stage. She also previously served as a member of the governing body of GMIT, before its transition to ATU, is a former chair of the Mayo Cancer Support Association, and serves on the Local Enterprise Office’s evaluations and appeals committee. Commenting on her appointment, Walsh said, “It is a privilege to lead Ibec and represent Irish business at a time of considerable change for our economy. “The resilience that has been synonymous with the Irish economy over the last number of years in the face of considerable global disruption is both a source of pride and a reminder that complacency is the enemy of our future success. “Operating from the west of Ireland, I know that the Irish economic success story is not just confined to our major cities, but one that touches towns and villages throughout the country.” She added: “When it comes to regional development, we must keep Ireland’s momentum up by expanding our infrastructure – from housing and energy to transport and digital connectivity, all of which will be key to enabling our continued growth in all parts of the country. “Individually, businesses of all sizes are also navigating this generation’s major technological disruption, but by supporting businesses to embrace artificial intelligence and develop future-focused skills, Ireland can be a real net beneficiary here.” Ibec CEO Danny McCoy said the group is “delighted” to have Walsh on board, and he commended her track record as an entrepreneur and business leader, alongside her “deep commitment to regional enterprise and digital innovation”, which he said gives her an exceptional perspective on the opportunities and challenges facing Irish businesses today. He added: “Through her leadership of Ibec’s digital and AI policy group, and her former role as regional president of Ibec West, Maureen has already demonstrated a clear commitment to shaping policy that serves businesses of all sizes and locations. “We look forward to working closely with Maureen during her term and wish her every success.” Don’t miss out on the knowledge you need to succeed. American Medical Association Fires Back Against Paper Finding AI Is Now Outperforming Doctors
2026-09-04

American Medical Association Fires Back Against Paper Finding AI Is Now Outperforming Doctors

Futurism | 2026-09-04

Visit Original Website
We have updated our Privacy Policy. Please review to learn more. By continuing to use our services, you agree to these updates. A new paper published in the Journal of the American Medical Association this month concluded, after surveying hundreds of studies on AI in medicine, that not only is AI already an indispensable tool that supports doctors, but that it’s in many ways surpassing them, and will soon be able to care patients on its own better than a human physician could. “In cognitive medical functions, AI-alone medical care is likely to be better than physician-only or physician-AI hybrid care,” the authors, the widely renowned bioethicist Ezekiel Emanuel and venture capitalist Vinod Khosla, flatly declare. But many physicians aren’t having it, Wired reports. One of the paper’s chief critics is the CEO of the American Medical Association himself, John Whyte. According to Whyte, some of the studies it surveyed are simulations, instead of blinded experiments, the gold standard of medical studies. And some contradict the author’s conclusions, such as a Nature article from February that found that most patients had a hard time talking to AI chatbots to get the expertise they needed, he argued. “The AMA does see the potential in these tools, but they have to be utilized in the context of a care plan that’s governed by a physician,” Whyte told Wired. The controversial study cites the rapid progress of LLMs since being publicly introduced in November 2022 with the release of ChatGPT. In spite of the tech’s relatively young age, the paper claims it already surpasses doctors at five fundamental tasks, which include: eliciting relevant medical information from patients, diagnosing the patient and conducting diagnostic testing, and managing chronic diseases. This gap between physicians and AI will widen because AI is “rapidly improving,” it predicts, while human doctors have their own capabilities diminished, or are “deskilled,” by becoming dependent on the very same AI models. “We’re making a prediction for four years from now,” Emanuel told Wired. “You don’t think that [AI is] going to be way more advanced than a doctor?” Taking this to its logical endpoint, the paper makes another bold claim: human physicians are better off not getting in AI’s way, finding that human-AI hybrids “actually degrade performance compared with AI alone.” “Thus, as AI improves, having humans in the loop will likely worsen patient care,” it adds. There’s a kernel of truth to this. AI-deskilling is already happening, with senior doctors warning of how med students are already heavily relying on AI tools. To be fair to the med students, though, so are actual doctors: internal data from OpenEvidence, an AI chatbot specifically designed for clinicians, suggests that around two thirds of physicians in the US are using the tool. New ground in AI in medical applications, meanwhile, is being broken seemingly every day. Last week, a team of neurosurgeons in the UK announced that they’d used an AI tool that guided them as they performed surgery to remove a tumor from a patient’s brain. Robert Wachter, head of medicine at the University of California, San Francisco (and a friend of Emanuel), cautions that AI and humans together are better for now, but that in the future, there “will be times when humans will muck up the performance,” he told Wired. Still, he emphasized that human doctors will always be irreplaceable. He called the idea of AI automating doctor’s jobs an example of the “doorman fallacy”: the fear that doormen would be made obsolete with the introduction of automatic doors. Doormen stuck around, accepting packages while also providing an air of hospitality that a robotic door can’t. The idea that highly trained doctors who spend years of their life honing their craft will become a bougie novelty like the doorman, somehow, is meant to be comforting. In any case, it’s common to hear highfalutin claims to come from advocates of AI technology, and nearly as common to see holes poked in them. Advocates of AI in education declared victory when a much-hyped study concluded that ChatGPT can have a “large positive impact on improving learning performance,” for instance, but the study was later retracted by the publisher Springer Nature after it found major discrepancies in its analysis. College Graduate Employment Is Cratering Where Data Centers Are Being Built
2026-09-04

College Graduate Employment Is Cratering Where Data Centers Are Being Built

Futurism | 2026-09-04

Visit Original Website
We have updated our Privacy Policy. Please review to learn more. By continuing to use our services, you agree to these updates. Will AI supercharge the economy? Will the data centers powering it bring prosperity and provide paying jobs to the communities they’re built in? Probably not. New research shows that rapid rise of AI has instead coincided with “unusually high levels” of unemployment among college graduates in Texas, the Financial Times reports. Texas, crucially, is the state with the most data center under construction. Researchers at the Federal Reserve Bank in Dallas found that job postings in the state had dramatically fallen for AI-exposed fields since the release of ChatGPT, disproportionately affecting college graduates. By the first quarter of 2025, postings plummeted by 8 percent compared to jobs that were deemed less exposed to AI, it also found. “These dynamics suggest that recent college graduates, whose unemployment rate rose to unusually high levels during this period of rapid [generative AI] adoption, are where the effects of GenAI on employment and earnings are likely to first appear,” the researchers wrote in the analysis, as quoted by the FT. Until recently, Texas and its governor Greg Abbott has embraced the AI data center buildout with open arms. A recent Reuters analysis found that requests from data centers and other large power users to connect to the state’s power grid soared from about 48 gigawatts in 2023 to more than 474 gigawatts currently, making it one of the fastest growing hubs for AI and cloud computing facilities. But as public backlash against data centers has continued to mount — and with midterm elections looming — Abbott changed his tune and became an AI industry critic, announcing a freeze on new data center connections last month. Along with the myriad concerns around the data center’s environmental impact, many fear that the technology that the data centers support will destroy jobs, while the facilities themselves provide little for local economies. The latest research, its authors say, demonstrates the reality of how AI is shaping labor demand in the very area that should be directly benefiting from it. It’s not the only research suggesting AI is wiping out entry level jobs — an outcome that industry figures like Anthropic CEO Dario Amodei have warned would happen. A recent analysis by researchers at Stanford’s Digital Economy Lab similarly found a marked decline in employment among young knowledge-workers, while the headcount of those working in fields not considered vulnerable to AI automation, including industrial and retail work, rose. “For 22 to 25 year olds, employment is falling in the most AI-exposed jobs and rising in the least exposed jobs,” one of the report authors said. There’s still a lot of uncertainty around how many jobs are truly being replaced by AI, and whether some of the chaos can be explained as AI being used as an excuse to fire workers for other reasons. But it’s brought a demoralizing effect on the workforce. In a Gallup poll conducted over the final three months of 2025, 72 percent of respondents said it was a “bad time” to find a good job. Uber Abruptly Ends Operations in Africa, Abandoning Drivers and Riders Mid-Trip

Futurism | 2026-09-04

Visit Original Website
We have updated our Privacy Policy. Please review to learn more. By continuing to use our services, you agree to these updates. Uber drivers and their passengers across Nigeria and Uganda were shocked on Wednesday when the platform abruptly ceased operations in the middle of the day — and, in some cases, even mid-trip. According to reporting by the Register, the “Uber rapture,” as it’s come to be known, came with little warning — an email notice sent on the very same day. “We are writing to share some difficult news,” Uber’s email to Nigerian users went, per a screenshot obtained by the outlet TechCabal. “After a thorough review of our business, we have made the tough decision to wind down our operations in Nigeria, effective 2 September, 2026.” Of course, the deluge of corporate communiques delivered via email in this day and age means an important memo like this could be easily ignored, if not relegated to the spam folder automatically. Case in point, the same-day memo did little to prepare drivers or riders for the deactivation, prompting waves of outrage on social media when the Uber app was shut off, seemingly out of nowhere. “The Uber rapture is wild! Drivers and riders were mid-trip when it happened,” wrote Nigerian journalist Anita Eboigbe in a post on social media. “Insane!” Another commentator wrote that a “friend of mine said they were on a ride when they got the message. She told the driver and he said ‘it’s a lie!’ The drivers who depend on it for livelihood had absolutely no idea.” “The app shut down mid-trip,” another account located in Nigeria explained in a comment on X-formerly-Twitter. “They never gave a heads-up or hinted at them leaving the landscape. I had done a delivery that same morning and about an hour later, we all got emails saying they were leaving immediately.” It’s a dramatic step for Uber, which had been operating in Nigeria since 2014, and Uganda’s capital city of Kampala since 2016. “This decision is limited strictly to these two markets and does not impact our operations across the rest of the continent,” the rideshare company said in a statement to the Register. “Uber remains deeply committed to sub-Saharan Africa, where we continue to see robust growth and long-term opportunity.” More on transit: Uber Drivers Say Waymo Robotaxis Are Driving Down Their Pay

While most of the vandals ripping down automatic license plate readers prefer to go incognito, an Albuquerque character named Jevon Martinez isn’t afraid to show his face. Weeks after Bernalillo county officials cancelled their contract with the controversial surveillance company Flock earlier this year, Martinez noticed the automatic license plate readers (ALPRs) were still up. In a series of videos posted on social media and flagged by KOB4, Martinez filmed himself tearing the camera towers down in order to return them to their rightful owners. “Since [Bernalillo County Sheriff’s Department] is busy — I know BCSO is busy, everyone who works for BCSO, I know you guys are busy, so I went ahead and helped you out,” Martinez says while driving his pickup truck, the bed of which is notably full of ALPR parts. “We got a bunch of these bad boys,” he says in another video, showing off a truck bed stuffed to the brim with tower parts. “Let’s see how much I can get in one day. That’s gonna be the challenge for everyone out there.” Playing the helpful citizen, Martinez drove his literal truckload of ALPR bits directly to the Sheriff’s department, where he was promptly taken into custody. Though he wasn’t armed and hadn’t committed any violent offense, officers made a dramatic show of force as they arrested him. Clad with ballistic-resistant plate carriers, the deputies surrounded Martinez’s truck, drawing their guns and forcing him into cuffs. “Is this a crime? United Nations Concedes That Efforts to Arrest Climate Change Have Failed, and the Planet Is Now Spiraling Into Environmental Catastrophe
2026-09-03

United Nations Concedes That Efforts to Arrest Climate Change Have Failed, and the Planet Is Now Spiraling Into Environmental Catastrophe

Futurism | 2026-09-03

Visit Original Website
We have updated our Privacy Policy. Please review to learn more. By continuing to use our services, you agree to these updates. With so many climate projections and mitigation strategies eyeing far-away years like 2050, it can be tempting to assume there’s still time to reverse the worst impacts of an ever-warming planet. Unfortunately, that’s no longer in the cards — at least according to the United Nations Environment Programme, which just released a dismal report calling for the “world to face the inevitability of exceeding global warming of 1.5 degrees Celsius” above pre-industrial levels. That’s significant, because the 1.5 degree threshold has long been associated with a range of grim climate tipping points at which the planet will experience irreversible consequences such as the “breakdowns of major ocean circulation systems, abrupt thawing of boreal permafrost, and collapse of tropical coral reef systems,” per a UN climate explainer. The new UN paper, coming on the heels of the hottest summer on record and pessimistically titled “Limiting Overshoot,” identifies the consequences of this reality, and recommends some immediate actions to keep things from spiraling even further. Though most scientists agree we’re hovering around the 1.4 degree point at the moment, the UN notes that even in the best case scenario, where “all climate commitments and policies are implemented, warming is projected to reach 1.8 degrees Celsius at a minimum.” If we follow our current track, however, the report highlights the distinct possibility of hitting 2.6 degrees by the year 2100. That catastrophic future is far from certain, although drastic intervention is becoming increasingly urgent if we hope to stave it off. Underscoring the severity of the present moment, the UN frames the situation in terms of maintaining warming at the 1.5 degree mark — no longer a theoretical ceiling, but the new floor going forward. Doing so, the report authors write, depends “on achieving sustained net-negative CO2 emissions at global scale in the second half of this century, and potentially further reductions of non-CO2 emissions.” “Decisions taken this decade will determine whether viable options remain to gradually reverse warming back to 1.5 degrees Celsius,” they explain. Speaking to CBC News, UN Environment Programme executive director Inger Andersen said “let’s be very clear, 1.5 target is still the goal.” “But now we need to approach it differently from above,” Andersen explained. “Every tenth of a degree we avoid still means less damage.” More on climate change: It’s Official: This Is the Hottest America Has Ever Been

China Installing Huge Facility That Turns Plastic Waste Into Jet Fuel
2026-09-03

China Installing Huge Facility That Turns Plastic Waste Into Jet Fuel

Futurism | 2026-09-03

Visit Original Website
We have updated our Privacy Policy. Please review to learn more. By continuing to use our services, you agree to these updates. Around the world, the demand for cleaner alternatives to petroleum-based jet fuel is rising. By 2030, the European Union will require a minimum sustainable aviation fuel (SAF) blend of 6 percent, which will rise to 70 percent by 2050. Countries like the United Kingdom, Singapore, Japan, and Brazil are instituting similar policies, putting pressure on players in the global energy trade to ease reliance on crude oil. SAF can come from all kinds of recycled waste byproducts like plant material, animal fat, and used cooking oil — the latter of which has already become a major source for existing SAF supplies. Unfortunately, the flow of extra spent fry oil is already slowing to a trickle as refiners swoop in to secure the gloop. That’s where Chinese company Niutech comes in. In a new press release, first spotted by Interesting Engineering, Niutech announced a massive contract with an anonymous “global energy major” to build out an “industrial continuous waste plastic pyrolysis production line” capable of churning out 10,000 to 50,000 tons of plastic-derived SAF each year. That might seem like a lot to unpack, but it’s really pretty simple. Basically, the Chinese firm is announcing the development of a massive supply line that turns plastic garbage into usable jet fuel. It plans to do so using a thermal decomposition process called pyrolysis, in which plastic waste is heated to extraordinary temperatures in an oxygen-free environment. Though it isn’t new, pyrolysis remains something of a niche for the industrial-grade plastics-recycling space. For this approach to meet the growing need for SAF, it first has to graduate beyond the realm of small eco-startups into a widely-accepted industry standard — which appears to be Niutech’s goal. Unlike previous SAF initiatives involving plastics, this particular production line will all be under one roof, cutting down on the need for costly shipping and handling. The Niutech refinery is also said to make use of a smorgasbord of plastics like polyethylene, nylon, polypropylene, and ABS, all “without complex pre-treatment such as washing, sorting or drying” — streamlining the complex process of sorting plastic and converting it to refinable oil at this kind of scale. Should Niutech and its anonymous industry partner succeed in bringing the system online, it would undoubtedly serve as a constant source of plastics-based SAF for the global aviation industry, and as a role model for other refineries to follow. “Large-scale pyrolysis projects impose stringent requirements on continuous operational capability,” a Niutech spokesperson said in the company’s press release. “Niutech has focused on pyrolysis technology for nearly 40 years and brings mature equipment and extensive project experience. This partnership is another validation of the strength of our industrial continuous technology.” More on China: American Tech Companies Are Suddenly Sweating Bullets as China Catches Up on AI

CD sales are making an unexpected comeback amid a retro tech boom
2026-09-04

CD sales are making an unexpected comeback amid a retro tech boom

TechCrunch | 2026-09-04

Visit Original Website
Disrupt 2026: OpenAI, Anthropic, Replit, and more take over 6 industry stages. 25% off tickets now Back by popular demand: Save up to $300 on Disrupt Retro tech is making a comeback, and this week, the Recording Industry Association of America (RIAA) put hard numbers to that trend in terms of music consumption habits. Surprisingly, the organization reported that CDs — yes, the shiny, round things that Spotify supposedly killed — are growing once again. According to the RIAA’s new report, CDs generated $171.1 million in revenue during the first half of 2026 — up 58.6% from roughly $107.9 million in the first half of 2025 — on 17.5 million units sold, up 45.7% from about 12 million units in the same period last year. That rebound is all the more interesting because it follows a rough 2025. Last year’s full-year report showed CD revenue had actually fallen 7.8% for the full year, from $338.9 million in 2024 to $312.4 million in 2025, while units sold dropped 11.6%, from 33.3 million to 29.5 million. The growth comes amid a surge of renewed interest in simpler tech, like so-called “dumbphones,” digital cameras, typewriters, landlines, and physical media like CDs and vinyl, among other categories. For Gen Z, the demand for these devices represents a sort of yearning for an era of tech they never got to experience — where you had more control over when you engaged with technology and how, and tech itself had less room to invade your life with its notifications, addictive apps, and algorithms. Several startups are playing in this space now, too, including landline makers like Tin Can, Ooma, and Pinwheel, and non-smartphones like those from Light, Dumb Co, Minimal, and others, including the soon-to-launch BlackBerry-like Clicks. Meanwhile, Gen Z consumers are snapping up old gadgets at thrift stores or on vintage tech websites like eBay or Retrospekt. And to be fair, the trend could be bigger than the RIAA’s numbers demonstrate, as they don’t include used CD sales, like those discs bought from thrift bins or garage sales, nor do they account for how many CDs have now been handed down from Gen X parents to their kids. We should note there’s one caveat to the trend here. The RIAA changed how it calculates dollar figures in 2025, switching from estimated retail value to wholesale value. That means the 2024 and 2025 revenue numbers aren’t directly comparable. But unit sales, which aren’t affected by that change, tell the same fundamental story: 29.5 million CDs sold in 2025, down from 33.3 million in 2024. So CDs were still declining before this year’s unexpected rebound. Vinyl sales continue to grow, too. In fact, so-called physical media revenue in the first half of 2026 jumped 25.9% to $731.5 million, driven ⁠not only by the 58.6% increase in CD revenue but also by a 17.7% increase in vinyl revenue. Krafton doubles down on India with another $250M bet beyond gaming
2026-09-04

Krafton doubles down on India with another $250M bet beyond gaming

TechCrunch | 2026-09-04

Visit Original Website
Disrupt 2026: OpenAI, Anthropic, Replit, and more take over 6 industry stages. 25% off tickets now Back by popular demand: Save up to $300 on Disrupt Krafton, the South Korean gaming company behind titles including PUBG and Battlegrounds Mobile India (BGMI), plans to invest another $250 million in India over the next three to four years as it pushes beyond gaming into AI, robotics, and deep tech. Announced Friday, the new commitment will take Krafton’s total planned investment in India to more than $500 million, including the over $250 million it had already invested in the country since 2021. The funds also come in addition to Krafton’s commitment to a $670 million India-focused growth fund it unveiled with South Korean internet conglomerate Naver and investor Mirae Asset in December 2025. At the time, Krafton said it would contribute around $137 million at the fund’s first close. The new $250 million pledge is separate from the growth fund, the company confirmed to TechCrunch. India has become a key market for Krafton, giving the gaming publisher access to the world’s most populous country, with more than a billion internet subscribers and over 700 million smartphone users. Krafton initially built its India business around gaming, led by BGMI, a revamped and localized version of PUBG that has surpassed 260 million downloads. In recent years, the company has also used investments in Indian startups to broaden its business beyond games. Krafton’s run in India has not always been smooth. The Indian government banned PUBG Mobile in 2020, along with hundreds of Chinese-linked apps, as tensions between New Delhi and Beijing escalated. Krafton stepped in after that and introduced BGMI in 2021. The company dropped Tencent Games as the game’s publisher in India and moved its India servers to Microsoft’s Azure cloud, changes that came as Krafton worked to address New Delhi’s concerns. However, in 2022, BGMI was itself banned in India, before the federal government allowed it to return on a three-month trial basis the following year. Krafton has continued to build around the franchise since its return and plans to launch BGMI Lite in India by the end of 2026. Despite the early setbacks, Krafton continued to invest in India and has so far backed about 18 companies, including four to five gaming studios, the company confirmed to TechCrunch. Its investments include Nodwin Gaming, Loco, Pratilipi, Kuku FM, and One Impression, among others. The new investment commitment comes after Krafton chairman Chang Byung-gyu met Indian Prime Minister Narendra Modi in New Delhi earlier on Friday. The two discussed opportunities for India and South Korea to work more closely across gaming, technology, and other emerging sectors, Krafton said. Krafton has also been building out its local operations. Last year, it acquired Pune-based Nautilus Mobile, the studio behind the Real Cricket franchise. The company also recently launched KIGI Academy, an industry-academia program aimed at training talent in game development and digital creation. Google’s Gemini Spark can now manage your Google Photos library
2026-09-04

Google’s Gemini Spark can now manage your Google Photos library

TechCrunch | 2026-09-04

Visit Original Website
Disrupt 2026: OpenAI, Anthropic, Replit, and more take over 6 industry stages. 25% off tickets now Back by popular demand: Save up to $300 on Disrupt Google is integrating more of its services with AI, as the company announced that its personal agent, Gemini Spark, can now manage your Google Photos library. That means you can ask Gemini Spark to execute tasks in Google Photos, like editing images, curating albums, automatically creating shared albums with your favorite shots, turning concert flyer photos into calendar appointments, running workflows, and more. On Thursday evening, Google Photos lead Shimrit Ben-Yair shared the new capabilities in a post on X, noting that the new capabilities would roll out over the next few weeks to eligible Gemini AI Pro and Ultra subscribers in the U.S. in English. The company didn’t say if or when it would roll out more broadly to international markets. For a while now, I’ve relied on @GeminiApp and @antigravity agents across a wide range of use cases—spanning creativity, productivity, and analysis. But I’ve always dreamed of having a power agent to help me get the most out of my 143,206 photos and videos. And that day has come!… pic.twitter.com/WbnkYpattQ The addition is yet another example of Google attempting to find product-market fit in terms of making AI appealing to consumers — in this case, by automating tedious tasks and helping people manage what are now often sizable photo libraries. The move comes at a time when the AI industry is reckoning with the fact that it hasn’t properly sold the promise of AI to consumers. This week, OpenAI CEO Sam Altman told Bloomberg that the industry has not “done a terrible job” communicating the benefits of technology, which has led to backlash from communities around the world. Announcements like this from Google are part of the problem. While they may make certain tasks easier, on their own, such features don’t seem revolutionary or even, really, necessary. (It’s not that difficult to make a photo album after all.) But the competitive nature of the AI industry is pushing companies to promote every AI-infused upgrade, no matter how small, instead of waiting until the company can tell a broader story about how AI has shaped its software for the better. To use Gemini Spark with Google Photos, you’ll need to first connect Google Photos to Gemini, then toggle on Spark in the top corner of the Gemini app, and enter your prompt. US military disabled ad tracking on troops’ devices following reports of targeted attacks
2026-09-04

US military disabled ad tracking on troops’ devices following reports of targeted attacks

TechCrunch | 2026-09-04

Visit Original Website
Disrupt 2026: OpenAI, Anthropic, Replit, and more take over 6 industry stages. 25% off tickets now Back by popular demand: Save up to $300 on Disrupt The U.S. Department of Defense has disabled advertising tracking on troops’ phones and computers as part of an effort to protect them from threats that target their locations, according to a letter shared with Sen. Ron Wyden. Per a letter shared with the senior Democrat on the Senate Intelligence Committee, Wyden said that the U.S. Army, Air Force, Navy, and Marine Corps, and Special Operations Command have all disabled advertising tracking across their government-issued devices. This includes the departments’ iPhones and Android devices, and Windows computers managed across the federal military enterprise network, the letter said. Reuters, which first reported the news and the letters, said the military rolled out the protections earlier this year. The Air Force told Wyden it implemented its changes more recently in July. The aim is to prevent adversaries, including hostile governments, from using location data derived from the apps on troops’ phones to target them on the battlefield or on their bases. Location data collected from phone apps is commonly shared with third-party companies and data brokers, which then sell the information on the commercial market. Disabling the advertising ID makes the person far more difficult to identify as the location data blends in with everyone else whose advertising ID is also disabled. Wyden raised the issue earlier this year in a letter to U.S. military leaders after finding that unnamed foreign adversaries had targeted U.S. troops in the Middle East using their commercially obtained location data. The senator, a known privacy hawk on Capitol Hill, said that while the move to reduce the advertising tracking was commendable, he warned that the personal devices of troops and contractors brought onto military bases could still expose service members and facilities to attacks. While the U.S. government has acknowledged that its troops face a risk from this data, the U.S. intelligence community and the FBI have confirmed that they buy this data for surveillance and intelligence collection without a warrant. Feds launch investigation into Tesla’s Cybercab deployment
2026-09-04

Feds launch investigation into Tesla’s Cybercab deployment

TechCrunch | 2026-09-04

Visit Original Website
Disrupt 2026: OpenAI, Anthropic, Replit, and more take over 6 industry stages. 25% off tickets now Back by popular demand: Save up to $300 on Disrupt The United States’ top automotive safety regulator has opened an investigation into Tesla’s decision to launch its new Cybercab on public roads with no steering wheel or pedals. The National Highway Traffic Safety Administration (NHTSA) announced Friday morning that it opened the probe mere hours after Tesla put the first Cybercabs on the streets of Austin, Texas. Federal vehicle safety regulations require manual controls like brake pedals, though the Department of Transportation recently proposed removing those requirements for vehicles that are designed to be autonomously driven. “NHTSA fully supports the safe development and deployment of automated vehicles. But as the federal regulator, we need to ensure that all of our laws are followed,” administrator Jonathan Morrison said in a statement. “Our approach of balancing innovation with safety oversight will allow the United States to maintain its global leadership in AV innovation.” NHTSA said Friday that Tesla told the agency it self-certified the Cybercab as being compliant with all of the Federal Motor Vehicle Safety Standards (FMVSS). Automakers traditionally self-certify whether their vehicles comply with FMVSS rules. In the filing, the agency said it was opening the investigation to “examine the process and technical data on which Tesla relied when certifying the Cybercab and related issues.” NHTSA said it will consider the extent to which the company’s certification depended on determinations that certain federal motor standards are inapplicable to the Cybercab. There is precedent to NHTSA’s inquiry. In 2022, Amazon-owned autonomous vehicle company Zoox self certified its cube-like robotaxi, which lacks traditional controls such as a steering wheel and pedals. NHTSA then opened up what it called a “special order” seeking more information from Zoox and formally launched an audit query — the same process it is now using for Tesla — the following the year. While Zoox was still heavily in its testing phase at that time, the investigation did slow its path to commercialization. Zoox had long maintained that the self certification process was sufficient and in 2025 the federal agency gave the company an exemption to demonstrate — not commercially operate — its technology. Zoox then went through the official process and filed for a temporary Part 555 exemption from eight Federal Motor Vehicle Safety Standards in hopes it would get the final OK that would allow it to charge for robotaxi rides. Zoox received final approval for that exemption in July 2026, eliminating one of the last remaining regulatory hurdles the company needed to clear before launching a commercial robotaxi service. The exemption did place some limits on Zoox. Under the temporary exemption, Zoox can add 2,500 vehicles per year to its commercial fleet, over the next for two years. Zoox opened up its commercial service several weeks later and now charges for rides in Las Vegas. Whether Tesla will face a similarly long process is unclear. Zoox navigated the regulatory process under the Biden and Trump administrations. On Friday, NHTSA acknowledged that it is in the process of changing some parts of the FMVSS that relate to manual controls in order to “unleash American innovation and enhance safety on our roads.” The agency said it “looks forward to finishing these critical updates and removing unnecessary barriers to American AV innovation in the coming months. Until that work is completed, however, existing standards remain in force.” This story has been updated with new information from NHTSA. How to Support Someone With Postpartum Depression
2026-09-04

How to Support Someone With Postpartum Depression

TIME | 2026-09-04

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings When Iza Thiago-Munoz brought her first baby home, she instituted a rule: She was the only person allowed to carry the baby upstairs. At the time, she lived in a narrow Philadelphia rowhouse with a steep staircase. If anyone else—including her husband—wanted to take the baby up, they had to buckle the infant into a car seat first. Her husband occasionally tried to reason with her. He could hold the baby tightly, he pointed out. “But what if you fall down the stairs?” she would reply. She didn’t know it at the time, but Thiago-Munoz was struggling with postpartum depression and anxiety, which amplified her lifelong tendency to imagine the worst-case scenario. She also felt enormous pressure to be a perfect mother. “I thought I had to do everything,” she says. Otherwise, she worried, “people would just think that I wasn’t good enough.” Asking for help felt like admitting that they were right. Thiago-Munoz is now a lactation consultant, postpartum doula, and founder of Clementina Health, a postpartum and lactation telehealth company. Her work is informed by what she experienced after giving birth—and by what she wishes the people around her had understood. “I just didn’t feel joy in anything,” she says. “I felt like I was supposed to be happy because, wow, this major thing just happened. And I was happy, but I wasn’t joyful.” Supporting someone with postpartum depression starts with knowing how it can show up, even when it doesn’t match the image you have in your head. Both the “baby blues” and postpartum depression can cause sadness, tearfulness, irritability, and feeling overwhelmed after giving birth, says Dr. Kelli Burroughs, an ob-gyn and vice chair of clinical affairs and patient experience at the University of Texas Medical School in Houston. But the baby blues are generally mild, begin within the first week after delivery, and improve within about two weeks. Postpartum depression lasts longer and interferes more significantly with daily life. Signs can include a persistently depressed mood; losing interest in things that usually bring joy; withdrawing from other people; difficulty eating, concentrating, or sleeping even when the baby is asleep; extreme fatigue; and feeling disconnected from the baby. Postpartum depression can also begin months after giving birth—not only during those first blurry weeks at home. It doesn’t always look the way people expect, either. Sometimes, the moms who are suffering the most appear to have everything under control, says Jayme Scarfo, a licensed professional counselor in Surprise, Ariz., who specializes in working with pregnant and postpartum women. They’re showering, cooking, going to the gym, and insisting: “It’s hard, but don’t worry. I’ve got it.” You don’t need to decide whether someone meets the diagnostic criteria before checking in. Describe what you’ve noticed without labeling her, Scarfo suggests: “I know this is a huge adjustment, and you haven’t seemed like yourself lately. How are you really doing?” Or: “You seem much more anxious than usual, and I’m worried about you. Can we talk about it?” Consider who should initiate that conversation, too. “The best person to ask those hard questions is often going to be whoever she feels the least pressure to perform for,” Scarfo says. That might be a sister, best friend, therapist, or fellow mom—not necessarily her partner or mother. Burroughs also likes asking questions that are harder to brush off with a reflexive “I’m fine.” For example: “Are you having more good days or bad days?” It’s also helpful to ask when she last slept for more than two hours or ate a real meal sitting down. Those answers can help you understand where she’s struggling—and how you might lighten the load. When someone declares they’re a “terrible mother,” your instinct might be to disagree. Of course you’re not! You’re doing an amazing job. But depression and anxiety symptoms can start to feel like part of someone’s identity, says Emma Basch, a clinical psychologist in Washington who specializes in perinatal mental health. Trying to prove her wrong might leave her feeling even more misunderstood. Instead of correcting her, acknowledge how much she’s hurting: “It must be so painful to feel that way. I’m here for you.” Burroughs likes: “I’m glad you told me. I’m glad you feel comfortable confiding in me.” That signals that you can handle the truth, and that your friend doesn’t need to resume pretending everything is fine. Skip the sunny reassurances, like “Enjoy every moment,” “They’re only this little once,” and especially “You just had a baby—you should be happy.” Those comments suggest she can simply choose her way out of what she’s experiencing. “Postpartum depression is a true medical condition,” Burroughs says. “This is not a sign of weakness. This is out of the control of the new mom.” Loved ones should try to remain calm if a new mom shares an intrusive thought, Scarfo says. A frightening flash like “What if I drop the baby?” isn’t the same as wanting it to happen. An alarmed reaction—or immediately suggesting an intervention like hospitalization—could make her less likely to confide in you again. Acknowledge that the thought sounds scary, and gently ask more: “How often are you having these thoughts?” “Do you feel like you might act on them?” “Do you feel safe right now?” “Who can we call together?” You don’t need to act like the thought is no big deal; it clearly feels like a very big deal to her. The goal is to become a safe place for her to say the things that aren’t picture-perfect, Scarfo says—and then help her figure out what she needs next. Talking about suicide or harming the baby does require immediate intervention, Basch says. The same is true of signs of postpartum psychosis, which can include hallucinations, delusions, paranoia, confusion, or a decreased need for sleep accompanied by a surge of energy. “It’s not a layperson’s job to diagnose,” she says, “but it’s a layperson’s job to take action.” Anyone experiencing a mental-health crisis can call or text 988. The National Maternal Mental Health Hotline, available at 1-833-TLC-MAMA, also provides free, confidential support 24/7 to pregnant and postpartum women and their families. “Let me know if you need anything” might sound kind. But it puts the burden back on the person who’s already overwhelmed. She hears “ball’s in your court,” Scarfo says, and “a depressed mom is not picking up the ball. She has 17 other things to do.” Instead, tell your loved one exactly what you’d like to do, Basch advises. “I’m dropping off dinner tonight—does 6 p.m. work?” is much easier to answer than “Can I bring you something sometime?” She can still say no or suggest another time, but she doesn’t have to identify a need, invent a solution, and delegate it to you. Be careful not to turn helping into unwanted visits. Scarfo and her sister-in-law used to leave meals on each other’s porches and say: “I’m dropping off dinner at 6. I’m going to leave it on the porch, and I’ll text you when it’s there.” There was no expectation that the recipient would invite anyone inside or make conversation. “It doesn’t put that burden of gratitude on the new mom,” Scarfo says—“that burden of performing or hosting.” Professional treatment might include therapy, medication, or both. Yet finding a provider, staying on top of insurance, making calls, and arranging child care can feel impossible when someone is already overwhelmed. Basch has seen loved ones do that legwork themselves and say: “I found a few therapists who specialize in postpartum mental health. I’d be happy to help you connect with one.” You might offer to make the call, accompany your loved one to an appointment, or watch the baby while she goes. If your partner keeps insisting that she’s fine but you remain concerned, Burroughs suggests contacting her ob-gyn, midwife, or other maternal-care provider yourself. Be specific about what you’ve observed: persistent sadness, repeated crying, withdrawal, difficulty bonding with the baby, or struggling to complete essential tasks. You might say: “I’m concerned about my partner’s emotional recovery after giving birth. These are the changes I’ve noticed at home.” The provider can then reach out to the patient without necessarily revealing who called, Burroughs says. They’ll usually open by saying: “I’m just calling to check on you. I want to see how your recovery is going.” Then, they’ll ask how the new mom feels emotionally on a scale of 1 to 10, whether she’s bonding with the baby, how often she feels sad, and whether she’s having more good days or bad ones. Those specific questions can make it harder for serious symptoms to disappear behind “I’m fine.” Support tends to pour in immediately after a baby is born, when everyone wants to deliver a casserole and tickle the newborn’s toes. Then people return to their own lives, even though the need for support often outlasts that initial flurry of attention. Continue checking in without demanding a response: “Thinking of you today. No need to text back.” Keep offering specific help, even if your loved one turns you down the first time. Scarfo suggests making it clear that you’ll come over whether she wants to run an errand alone or lie down on the couch while somebody else handles the chaos around her. Thiago-Munoz still remembers the neighbor who noticed that she had stopped taking her dog on his usual 9 a.m. walk. If Thiago-Munoz hadn’t appeared by 9:15, her neighbor came over and walked him herself. “I honestly never would have asked her to take the dog for a walk,” she says. “I didn’t have the capacity to look for someone to do it. She knew my routine.”

AI Will Transform Work, But It Can't Replace Relationships
2026-09-04

AI Will Transform Work, But It Can't Replace Relationships

TIME | 2026-09-04

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings AI is everywhere. It's reshaping industries, accelerating innovation, and changing how millions of people work. And while AI will transform work, it is crystal clear to me that AI can not replace relationships. Working in real estate has taught me this firsthand. Great real estate agents are more than just salespeople. Real estate agents help people as they build, and often rebuild, their lives. They act as crisis managers, trusted confidants during divorce, empty-nester therapists, grief counselors, life coaches, matchmakers, and above all, trusted advisors who guide clients through some of life's biggest transitions. This is not to say that the real estate sector is beyond disruption. In fact, it is a particularly turbulent industry. And yet, the role of real estate agents has survived global pandemics, market ups and downs, shifting mortgage rates, and every other major industry disruption. AI will be no different for real estate agents, or other relationship-based professions. To be sure, AI is reshaping how we all work. And many workers are understandably anxious. Relentless reports predicting job loss and economic disruption have perhaps unsurprisingly deepened public skepticism about AI, particularly among young adults and recent college graduates. Numerous commencement speakers who touted AI this graduation season were booed, including both Google’s former and current CEOs.Communities across the country are pushing back against the rapid expansion of AI data centers, raising legitimate concerns about rising energy demands, water consumption, and the environmental impact these massive facilities can have on the places they call home. Even Pope Leo XIV weighed in on the rise of AI, publishing his first encyclical letter, Magnifica Humanitas, which thoughtfully evaluates the future of humanity in the age of AI. All of these concerns have exacerbated the AI trust gap. However, once a transformative technology is unleashed on society, there's no putting the genie back in the bottle. When Netflix sunsetted DVDs and made it possible for people to stream movies from the comfort of their homes, people stopped going to Blockbuster. When Spotify put a million songs in our pockets, people stopped buying CDs. AI is also a service that can make people’s lives easier. For example, it's really good at writing code. In my own industry, AI has dramatically lowered the barrier to innovation by empowering real estate professionals to build software and workflows tailored to their unique business needs—something expensive, off-the-shelf software often fails to deliver. Before AI, if a real estate professional had an idea for a new tool or workflow, they typically needed to hire a software developer, secure funding, and wait months—sometimes even years—for a finished product. Today, that equation has changed. With AI and vibe coding, they can turn an idea into a working prototype in a matter of hours with a fraction of the financial investment once required. I have a colleague who owns a real estate brokerage that now builds all of its technology in-house. Using Claude, he has created dynamic market reports, interactive sales dashboards, and even a custom Gmail widget that allows his agents to access and update the company's CRM without ever leaving their inbox. There's no need to log into another application or juggle multiple browser tabs. This kind of change isn't unique to real estate. It’s happening in education, finance, manufacturing, and virtually every other industry. No matter your profession, you may spend tremendous amounts of time on administrative work: writing spreadsheet formulas, analyzing documents, sending email, producing marketing content, organizing information, preparing presentations, and tackling repetitive tasks that add little strategic value. But whether you're an attorney, consultant, plumber, financial advisor, or sales executive, your greatest value doesn’t come from routine administrative work. It comes from solving problems and building relationships. Tools like ChatGPT, Claude, and Gemini can analyze contracts, create reports, summarize research, review documents, and automate a plethora of other tedious tasks. They allow professionals to spend less time on administration and more time serving their clients and building meaningful relationships. But AI isn't replacing the qualities that matter most. AI can't demonstrate genuine empathy, navigate difficult conversations, inspire confidence, or build lasting relationships. It can't sit across the table from a family deciding whether to move across the country or coach a struggling employee. It can't read the room, recognize when someone is hesitant, or understand the deeply personal circumstances behind many of life's biggest decisions. Those are uniquely human capabilities and always will be. As AI becomes better at technical tasks, human skills become the differentiator. Communication, empathy, and trust will define the next generation of successful professionals. The future of work isn't choosing between people and AI. It's using AI to amplify the human experience. Because AI will transform work, but it can't replace relationship TIME Ideas hosts the world's leading voices, providing commentary on events in news, society, and culture. We welcome outside contributions. Opinions expressed do not necessarily reflect the views of TIME editors.

What a Record Hunting and Fishing Expansion Means for America’s Wildlife Refuges
2026-09-04

What a Record Hunting and Fishing Expansion Means for America’s Wildlife Refuges

TIME | 2026-09-04

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Snow geese fly over Bosque del Apache National Wildlife Refuge in New Mexico. Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings The Department of the Interior implemented a massive expansion of fishing and hunting access in the United States, opening 1,488 opportunities across 32 states. With the rule in effect as of Monday, more than 92 million acres, or 95% of National Wildlife Refuge System land, are now available for the practices. Expanded hunting and fishing are now allowed in areas where the practices were never before legal, including Rocky Flats National Wildlife Refuge in Colorado and Stewart Lake National Wildlife Refuge in North Dakota. The rule also permits hunting and fishing for additional species and expands some seasons at locations where those activities were already allowed. “America’s hunters and anglers now have something significant to look forward to in the fall,” said Interior Secretary Doug Burgum in the press release announcing the rule Aug. 13. “We are also promoting good government by reducing the regulatory burden and working with states to facilitate these outdoor activities.” Read More: Trump Administration Scales Back Endangered Species Protections—Again The U.S. Fish and Wildlife Service (FWS) oversees more than 570 national wildlife refuges that were “established to serve a statutory purpose that targets the conservation of native species dependent on its lands and water,” according to the agency’s website. Christopher Williams, a professor of wildlife ecology at the University of Delaware, says the refuges were initially created to protect wildlife and then later helped to combat “extreme market hunting,” or unregulated hunting of animals. They became safe habitats and breeding sanctuaries for endangered species under the 1966 Endangered Species Preservation Act. Now, hunting and other forms of recreation are permitted across a majority of those refuges when “compatible with each station’s purpose and mission under federal law,” per FWS. The very definition of a refuge seems to have “slowly morphed into an entity that was meant to provide an opportunity for hunting access,” Williams says. The new rule raises a question of compatibility: How can the refuges fulfill their conservation mandate amid the nation’s largest-ever expansion of hunting and fishing? The FWS said this week’s expansion is about “maximizing access where compatible with conservation goals.” It also said that the new rule will help revitalize rural economies, as hunters and anglers contribute more than $144 billion annually to the U.S. economy. “These activities support jobs, fund conservation efforts, and sustain outdoor traditions that connect communities to the land and to each other,” the August announcement read. Sporting groups touted the “historic expansion” and its potential ability to help retain hunters and fishers at these refuges, which may rely on them for curbing overabundant and invasive species. Williams says it makes sense that conservation groups support the expansion, since hunters and anglers play an “enormously important role in American wildlife conservation.” “And with long-term concerns about declining hunting participation, providing meaningful access remains an important part of recruitment, retention, and reactivation efforts,” he says. “BHA applauds all efforts to responsibly expand and improve public access for hunters and anglers and appreciates the Administration’s recognition of the important role sportsmen and women play in the conservation and stewardship of public lands and waters,” Backcountry Hunters & Anglers said in a statement when the expansion was proposed in May. This was not the first time the Trump Administration proposed such an expansion. A version of the rule from 2020 opened some 859 opportunities across 2.3 million acres. The increase in scope from his first term is about 73%. The new rule also makes more than 1,000 modifications to existing federal regulations that previously guided access to the refuges, including simplifications and deletions. The Trump Administration has said the sweeping revisions aim to make it easier to understand and enforce guidelines from state to state. But some conservationists have concerns about how the abrupt elimination of that many regulatory mandates, all at once, will affect the refuges. Robert Fischman, professor of environmental law at Indiana University Maurer School of Law, says that such a dramatic overhaul is worth noting. “I mean, 1,000 rules sounds pretty important,” he tells TIME. One significant change that comes with the new rule is a reversal on lead ammunition and tackle. Following a lawsuit in 2021, the government said it would phase out the potentially harmful materials and require hunters and anglers to switch to non-toxic alternatives. The Center for Biological Diversity sued FWS after it proposed an expansion on hunting opportunities during Trump’s first term. The lawsuit argued that the expansion could jeopardize protected species, which are susceptible to ingesting the lead ammunition and tackle. It also claimed that there would be residual harm to the greater ecosystem, as lead ammunition and tackle can contaminate fields and waterways. The lawsuit was settled under the Biden Administration in 2022, with FWS saying it would phase out the use of lead ammunition and tackle at several refuges across the country. Aaron Bloom, a senior attorney with nonprofit Earthjustice's Biodiversity Defense Program, said the phase-out was supposed to go into effect across eight refuges in September 2026. But with the new rule, the phase-outs will no longer take effect. In response to the reversal, Bloom says Earthjustice will be looking at litigation—as will other environmentalist groups. These phase-outs would have “protected both wildlife and human health, hunters and their families, from the adverse impacts of lead poisoning,” Bloom tells TIME. “There's absolutely no reason we should be allowing them in our wildlife refuges.” FWS staffing decreased by approximately 18% between April 2024 and May 2025, according to agency records obtained by the Center for Biological Diversity. The decline can be attributed in part to the “workforce optimization initiative” implemented by the Department of Government Efficiency at the beginning of 2025. Fischman and Williams both express concern about the staffing cuts and how they will impact oversight amid expanded hunting and fishing access. “What I'm hearing from people who are on the ground is you have whole national wildlife refuges that no longer have any staff there full-time,” Fischman says. “You have a refuge manager running around from one refuge to another, riding circuit to try to keep things together.” Fischman questions whether there are enough staff left to “really understand” and document the impacts of the new rule. He says that streamlining refuge-specific regulations makes enforcement easier and may partially compensate for staffing reductions; however, he is skeptical that states have enough staff to patrol the affected refuges and ensure that hunters and anglers are complying with the regulations that do still apply. “It’s dependent on there being people there to maintain trails, to monitor impacts on species, to enforce the law,” he says. “It could be that the rulemaking seems pretty logical [to the Administration], but on the ground can't be practically implemented in a way that is consistent with public safety and compatible with both the system mission and the refuge purposes.” According to Williams, it's all a balancing act. He points out the need to preserve conservation efforts while boosting the economies of rural communities and reversing a long-term decline in hunting participation in the U.S. “On one hand, are you reaching a point where you might be doing some harm of not having adequate sanctuary for wildlife? Or maybe you are making a net improvement to increase retention and recruitment of hunters, which provides a financial benefit?” he wonders. “The answer is probably yes on both sides of the house.”

Philippines' Vice President Sara Duterte Faces Arrest Over Alleged Grave Threat to President Marcos

Philippines' Vice President Sara Duterte Faces Arrest Over Alleged Grave Threat to President Marcos

Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Philippine Vice President Sara Duterte arrives before the start of her impeachment hearing at the Philippine Senate in Metro Manila on July 7, 2026. Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings A Philippine court on Friday ordered the arrest of Vice President Sara Duterte over alleged grave threats to her former ally, President Ferdinand “Bongbong” Marcos Jr., his wife, and a former House Speaker. The warrant against Duterte, 48, set the bail at 120,000 pesos ($1,900) for ​each of the three counts against her. “Regardless of the question on jurisdiction, she has no intention of evading the law and will continue to exercise all her legal remedies,” Duterte’s lawyer said in a statement. Duterte has been charged with threatening the lives of Marcos Jr., First Lady Liza Araneta-Marcos, and his cousin, former House Speaker Martin Romualdez. The case stems from a November 2024 virtual press conference where the Vice President revealed an apparent assassination plot against Marcos, his wife, and Romualdez. “I already talked to someone—I told him, If I’m killed, kill [Marcos], Liza Araneta, and Martin Romualdez,” she said in Filipino, “No joke, no joke. I already gave orders. If I die, I told them, ‘Don’t stop until they’re dead.’ And then he said, ‘Yes.’” The threat is also one of the allegations included in the impeachment complaint against Duterte, on top of alleged misuse of public funds as Vice President and during her stint as Education Secretary. Duterte has repeatedly denied any wrongdoing. The Vice President is currently standing trial at the Philippine Senate, serving as an impeachment court, following her second impeachment in May. If convicted in the impeachment court, she could be removed from office and barred from running in the 2028 national elections, where she said she plans to contest the presidency. Philippine law typically allows candidates to run for public office even if they face pending criminal charges. Duterte is the daughter of former President Rodrigo Duterte, who is awaiting trial before the International Criminal Court in The Hague, Netherlands, for the thousands of deaths attributed to his violent anti-drug campaign. The Duterte family still enjoys broad public support in the Philippines, despite the controversies. Recent surveys show Sara Duterte herself is the top preferred candidate by Filipinos to succeed Marcos, whom she had a falling out with. Marcos, who took office in 2022, is limited to a lone six-year term. The presidential office said in a statement that it “respects the decision of the court” to issue an arrest warrant. Jean Encinas-Franco, a political science professor at the University of the Philippines, tells TIME that “the law has finally caught up with the Vice President,” but questioned the timing of the arrest warrant’s release. Franco said Duterte’s arrest warrant could successfully repel criticisms that Marcos is a “weak” leader as the impeachment proceedings have dragged on. However, Franco says it remains unclear which political camp—if any—will benefit from the latest political development. “We don't know what will happen because it's still 1-½ years [until the elections] and that's a lifetime in Philippine politics.”
