Why You Shouldn’t Work on Labor Day
2026-09-04

Why You Shouldn’t Work on Labor Day

TIME | 2026-09-04

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings It’s Saturday morning and your boss emails you a question that can wait until Monday. What do you do? If you’re like me, you read the message while walking out of your yoga class and respond before lunch. This work culture makes the U.S. an outlier among high-income economies. Since 2017, countries including France, Belgium, Spain, Portugal, Ireland, Australia, Slovakia, Greece, and Argentina have legislated some form of a right to disconnect. The European Union is working on a policy that could take effect as soon as 2028. In Australia, employees have the right not to read or respond to employers outside of work hours. In Portugal, mid-size employers risk fines of over $10,000 for contacting employees when they’re off the clock. As we think about the employment landscape in the U.S. this Labor Day, we should consider a similar approach that protects the right of employees to not work when they’re not officially working. This could solve a lot of problems for families and communities while actually improving job performance. Since the pandemic, the boundaries between work and home have all but collapsed. With many workers still doing a lot of remote work, many of us send our first email early in the morning and our last late at night, creating what Microsoft calls the “infinite workday.” We squeeze in a load of laundry, parent-teacher conference, and family dinner somewhere in between. Being pulled in so many directions at the same time is awful for our health and the quality of our work, for a simple reason: The human brain can’t multitask. “Each time we are exposed to a new stimulus and engage in the sequential process of disconnecting and reallocating our attention, we are expending valuable metabolic energy,” University of California, Santa Barbara technology management professor Paul Leonardi writes in Digital Exhaustion: Simple Rules for Reclaiming Your Life. “Our attention is fragmented. We lose focus and become easily distracted. We can’t get things done as quickly or as easily as we should be able to, and often quality suffers.” Humans just didn’t evolve to keep switching between tasks as quickly and regularly as we do today, Leonardi warns. This explains why so many of us are exhausted and burned out. It also explains why we spend so much time working: We aren’t as efficient as we could be. When I don’t work on the weekend, I unquestionably show up as a better employee on Monday morning. That’s because spending time with friends and family, exercising, getting outdoors, and volunteering in my community leave me refreshed and energized. What’s more, when I get my kids’ permission slips filled out and the fruit and veggies chopped on the weekend, thoughts of unfinished business aren’t floating through my head while I’m working. That means I can achieve a state of what the psychologist Mihaly Csikszentmihalyi famously termed “flow,” getting completely absorbed in my work and reaching peak performance. Allowing workers to disconnect would therefore make them better employees. It could also solve several public policy problems. Our children need present caregivers. It only takes seconds for a child to fall or drown while we’re distracted by a manager’s latest missive. When parents spend more time on screens, their children also develop poorer language skills. And, of course, children are unlikely to open up about their problems while we’re distracted on our phones. Plus, if workers disconnected during their off-hours, they’d also have more time to nurture the offline connections that our country desperately needs. I would agree that we’re experiencing what psychologist Marisa Franco calls a “friendship famine.” Since 1990, the percentage of American adults who say they have no close friends has quadrupled, according to last year’s American Perspectives Survey. That is crushing. The quality of our friendships and social connections is one of the strongest predictors of our health, happiness, and how long we’ll live, according to the Harvard Study of Adult Development. It’s more important than things like our cholesterol, genes, and salaries. And being lonely is as toxic for our health as alcoholism or smoking. Of course, as Americans know one another less, we help one another less. This can explain why volunteerism has also been declining. That’s particularly sad because banding together has long been a central part of our national character, as Alexis de Tocqueville famously wrote in Democracy in America. It’s especially important in a country like ours where people can’t rely on a strong social safety net when they hit hard times. Labor Day is an obvious prompt for U.S. lawmakers to consider a right to disconnect. In the meantime, we can change our behavior and our workplaces. Employers should take the lead. Even without a national law, they should develop their own policies about disconnecting and publish them prominently on their websites so prospective employees can consider them even before accepting job offers. Volkswagen pioneered this approach in Germany in 2012 when it stopped sending emails to employees who weren’t senior managers 30 minutes after their shifts ended and resumed 30 minutes before their next shifts began. A series of studies published in 2021 found that when people receive non-urgent work emails outside of work hours, we tend to think we need to respond faster than the senders believe is necessary. Receiving these types of emails also increases our stress and decreases our wellbeing, but senders underestimate the degree to which their messages stress us out. Indicating in the email that a response wasn’t expected right away reduced the pressure on recipients. An even better strategy could be schedule-sending emails so colleagues receive them when they return to work. Another thing we can combat individually is the urge to check our messages every time we have a spare minute. Pulling out our phones is often a convenient way to avoid negative emotions or awkward encounters. Yet all this task switching leaves us distracted and depleted, and I can’t realistically handle any of the issues in the emails I read in the 30 seconds it takes to walk from my yoga class to my car. It stands to reason that our brains would be less foggy if we used those small slices of our lives to take a deep breath, notice our surroundings, and have short, pleasant interactions with the people around us. When we returned to our desks, we’d think more clearly, work more efficiently, and send better responses. This Labor Day, there’s a way for workers to be healthier, happier, more productive, and solve a lot of our public policy problems, too. We need to stop working when we’re not working. Earle Meets World Is One Long ‘Get Ready With Me’ Video
2026-09-04

Earle Meets World Is One Long ‘Get Ready With Me’ Video

TIME | 2026-09-04

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings “You’re gonna see everything on this show,” Alix Earle assures viewers in the voiceover that opens her Netflix reality series Earle Meets World. But if the show made a genuine effort to fulfill the 25-year-old social media star’s vow to reveal the rawest, most intimate aspects of her life, then the only reasonable conclusion to draw from it would be that there just isn’t much to see. As her fans (she has 8.5 million on TikTok alone) will anticipate, “everything” refers specifically to ongoing drama around Earle’s parents’ divorce, her own breakup with NFL player Braxton Berrios, and her mysterious falling-out with fellow creator and former boss Alex Cooper. All of this does indeed come up in the eight-episode series, now streaming in full, which tries mightily to position the large, blended Earle family as the Kardashian-Jenners for a new generation. Yet the bombshells teased in months’ worth of promotional materials aren’t especially explosive. Nor is there a breakout personality among a cast that, understandably enough, seems either too chill or too concerned with appearing likable to really entertain. Earle Meets World plays less like a juicy docusoap than like a tediously extended version of the Get Ready With Me (GRWM) videos that made its subject famous—one that suggests less overlap than you might expect between the qualities of a successful influencer and those required for reality TV stardom. Earle is one of those celebrities who are universally known to people under 30 but barely on the radar of anyone much older. (I’ll admit to having confused her with Cooper, another all-American blonde who parlayed internet fame into a Netflix series, and I know I’m not the only one.) Though I wouldn’t recommend making the show your first trip down the Alix Earle rabbit hole, here is a quick intro for the unfamiliar: Conventionally beautiful yet plagued by cystic acne, Earle grew her following as a University of Miami undergrad with mirror-facing GRWM TikToks, eventually going viral with a vulnerable post about her skin troubles. Then came the brand deals and new platforms. Cooper signed Earle, an obvious heir, to her podcast network; they abruptly parted ways in early 2025 and have been vaguely but publicly feuding ever since. Meanwhile, Earle has continued her ascent, launching a skincare brand, Reale Actives, and introducing herself to people elderly enough to watch network TV on Dancing With the Stars. From left: Sally, Alix Earle, and Ashtin Earle in Earle Meets World Netflix Synergistically positioned to bring her social media followers to a streaming platform that needs young subscribers to maintain its dominance while filling gaps in her own reach, Earle Meets World is a multigenerational family soap. Alix and 23-year-old Ashtin—a less-extroverted brunette whose TikTok audience still tops a million—are the It-girl sisters who constantly squabble but consider each other best friends. Their mom, Alisa, and dad, TJ, have been divorced for more than a decade, their split catalyzed by TJ’s affair with Ashley Dupré, a key character in the prostitution scandal that sank former New York governor Eliot Spitzer. Now Dupré is Ashley Earle, TJ’s wife and the mother of their three kids; the eldest, Izabel, is, at 13, already cranking out Outfit of the Day videos like the Gen Alpha Kylie Jenner she has the pedigree to become. A recent breast cancer survivor, Alisa has her own longtime partner, Todd. Her messy separation from TJ has evidently given way to a tenuous friendship between the couples, although it seems doubtful they spend as much time together off-camera as on. These relationships are further complicated by the role TJ, who made his fortune in construction, has assumed as Alix and Ashtin’s “dadager.” All the raw material for an unscripted family drama is present. Somehow, though, the fireworks tend to fizzle as soon as they’re lit. With the divorce so far in the past, a season’s worth of halfhearted conflict among the parents is wrung out of TJ joking that Alisa could be a “young piece of ass” on The Golden Bachelor. Ashley’s friction with Alix is framed as fallout from the latter’s history of oversharing about the former but feels more like standard stepfamily tension, heightened by reality-TV producers' prodding and Alix’s hypervigilance about her stepmom’s sometimes-awkward social media presence. Ironically, we only really witness the self-centered, control-freak behavior relatives are always attributing to Alix when she’s struggling to manage her image and brand identity. As a result, the most intense moments of the series tend to involve cringey TikToks or premature glimpses of Reale Actives packaging. You probably have to be a diehard Earle girl to find this stuff compelling. And even if you are, you might be disappointed by how many climactic Earle Meets World scenes, from Ashtin kicking Alix out of her New York apartment to Cooper goading Alix to come at her, unfold in now-ancient vertical videos that already have millions of views. It doesn’t help the show that the two people with whom Alix seems most preoccupied throughout the season—Cooper and Berrios—aren’t present. Instead, we get her side of these stories, conveyed in interviews and stiff conversations over cocktails at trendy, jewel-toned lounges, often so stripped of details that they come off as, respectively, a generic exploitative mentorship and your typical ambivalent breakup. A scene in the premiere that seems even more staged than the average docusoap grievance dump has Alix talking with TJ about Cooper in sentences that could’ve been scripted by a crisis-PR pro: “I definitely feel betrayed by Alex Cooper.” “She took advantage of my admiration for her.” Neither this storyline nor the Berrios one advances until the very end of the season, and even those late developments are anticlimactic. (Tabloid headlines linking Earle and Tom Brady, meanwhile, get coy winks but no substantive context.) From left: Ashley Earle, Thomas "TJ" Earle, Alisa Earle, and Todd in Earle Meets World Netflix Much of the blame for Earle Meets World’s slightness and superficiality, as well as the shapelessness of episodes that drag despite running less than 40 minutes apiece, should fall on the show’s producers. Not only have they failed to identify entertaining narratives within this family’s daily life; they’ve also made their desperation to do so too apparent in the final product. To be fair, they had a difficult assignment. As eager as they look to be on screen together, the Earles are—or act—too calm and conciliatory to drive reality-soap plots. No one wants to look unhinged, least of all Alix, who talks about being unstable but can’t risk her credibility as an entrepreneur, pitchwoman, and parasocial companion to millions by getting truly ugly on camera. Which is precisely what natural born reality stars have always done. Compare Alix and Ashtin’s nights at the club to the sloppy-drunk violence and infidelity of the 20-somethings on Jersey Shore and Vanderpump Rules, or her parents’ relationships to chaotic Real Housewives marriages, and it all looks rather tame. No one here can quip like the best Bravo scrappers and Traitors debaters, either, so the show lacks the element of humor that suffuses every classic of the genre. Influencers fall into a cultural gray area between entertainment and entrepreneurship; most excel at one more than the other. Both fields demand storytelling skills. But that doesn’t mean a person with a preternatural ability to sell people the products they watch her apply to her face as she scatters breadcrumbs about her personal life necessarily has the makings of an unscripted drama’s protagonist. Earle might be too shrewd a businesswoman to have unadulterated main character energy.

The Missing Ingredient of Modern Life
2026-09-04

The Missing Ingredient of Modern Life

The Atlantic | 2026-09-04

Visit Original Website
Sign up for Ordinary Extraordinary, Ian Bogost’s newsletter on making everyday life vivid. You have, by now, probably come across the notion of maxxing—looks-maxxing, sleep-maxxing, protein-maxxing, potassium-maxxing, sun-maxxing, testosterone-maxxing. Etymologically speaking, maxxing seems to be a descendant of somewhat recent slang—for those of us who remember the late 20th century, “maxin’ and relaxin’ ” and “to the max” both come to mind—but in practice, maxxing is a reductionist worldview cloaked in expansionist language. Check out more from this issue and find your next story to read. The term is often invoked to describe a narrowly focused goal: If you’re maxxing, you’re likely trying to make yourself smaller, sharper, more chiseled. Maxxing requires sacrifice. It requires careful measurement. And in this way, maxxing is a consequence of our broader optimization-obsessed culture, a fixation fueled by every product, device, app, and service that trains people to collect and analyze data generated by their own behavior. This culture of optimization has been building for some time now, since at least the 19th century, when industrialism made efficiency a core ideal of working life. Later, when offices overtook factories as the emblem of work, efficiency became more abstract. Productivity began to be measured not only in profits maximized, but also in meetings shortened, messages answered, and bottlenecks removed. Over time, workers started managing themselves preemptively in accordance with the mandate of efficiency, optimizing their own calendars and lunch breaks. This optimization mindset leaked out of business and into everything else. People stopped asking what activities were for and started asking how to do them better, faster, or more completely. Ian Bogost: People are thinking about looksmaxxing all wrong Efficiency now infuses every aspect of contemporary life, if you let it. Instacart and DoorDash can replace grocery shopping and dining out. People optimize their vacations, maximizing credit-card points and airport-lounge strategies. Kindle streaks and Goodreads goals have turned books into items on a checklist. Wearables have turned exercise into VO₂ or HRV maximization. The more nutrition-obsessed among us have transformed eating into macronutrient acquisition. Every pleasure can become instrumental—with every instrument focused on the outcomes it might produce. Optimization is terrific if you want to get somewhere else in the future. But it’s terrible for being where you actually are right now. When goals overtake experience, you lose the freedom to encounter your life as it happens. And when you lose that ability, you are no longer living in full. We used to optimize work so we could do everything else. Now we optimize life as though living were another job. Too many of us seem to be maxxing our way out of the experiences and perspectives that give us maximum meaning. I’ve come to believe that wonder, the most immense of our sensations, is the antidote. Wonder can rise up anywhere. It might arrive after you hike to Hopi Point, nearly a mile above the Grand Canyon’s river floor. Or as you take in the northern lights from Tromsø, Norway. Or, on a different scale, wonder might be expected while you hold your newborn child for the first time, recognizing human life in its primal form and knowing that your own has now changed entirely. For children themselves, wonder remains abundant—in the bigness of a Tyrannosaurus rex in a museum, or the flicker of fireflies in the summer dusk. But for many adults, wonder is too often compartmentalized: a feeling we associate with extraordinary and rare experiences, banished to a realm outside the mundanities of daily life. The Wonder Reader: A weekly guide to Atlantic stories that will fascinate and delight For me, several aspects of modern life have made wonder seem out of reach lately: the rise of “frictionless” automated services for chores; the decline of serendipity in a world where every trip is Google Mapped; the ease with which everything can be done remotely, averting the happenstances and textures of regular routines; the ability to opt out of ambient noise (and impromptu conversations) by popping in AirPods; and, of course, our tendency to fill the gaps in our days by staring at our phones. But most of all, wonder feels scarce because contemporary life has depleted the value of the moment. Time has become instrumentalized, and as a result we have taught ourselves to treat the present primarily as a waiting room in which to plan for—and, yes, optimize—the future. Efficiency culture has muscled out the present so completely that it feels difficult to occupy a moment at all, let alone fully. Even the seasons, which help us mark the present amid the endless passage of time, seem to be anxious to overcome one another—pumpkin-spice creamers and “Pumking” imperial pumpkin ales now appear in supermarkets in July. Wonder interrupts that impulse to rush to the future before it arrives. It makes the world stop serving our self-centered plans or intentions, and instead offer something worth encountering for its own sake. For years, I have struggled with the problem myself. The urge to optimize caused me to flee to the future constantly. I realized that I felt best able to overcome efficiency culture when I treated supposedly unimportant sensations, objects, and events as vital ones instead: sliding my feet into the plush softness of slippers in the morning, feeling the warmth of a mug of coffee in my hands, hearing the gravel crunch under my feet as I walked. None of these acts compromised purpose or plans. All they required of me was to construe what I was already doing as valuable. Atlantic Reads: The Small Stuff with Ian Bogost Eventually I came to recognize that my long-standing obsession with ordinary things—including many that I have written about in The Atlantic, among them toasters, gas-station signs, backpacks, slushies, and gearshifts—offered a reason to take the present seriously. These objects show that beauty and even genius are often expressed through design choices and simple engineering. Tools we might think of as “useful” are in fact wondrous. The A BIT MORE button on modern toasters is a perfect example: so useful that you might overlook it as obvious, yet absolutely transformative in the making of toast. For some readers, this may generate an eye roll. Cynicism is pervasive, and not everyone has time for or interest in the scenic route. But I believe there are more of us wonder-seekers than the cynics and maxxers among us might like to admit. And besides: The route I am suggesting does not require an out-of-the-way trip. It simply asks you to accept gifts that the universe is already handing you. Wonder may not improve your résumé, make you hotter, or maximize your income. It will simply remind you that you are a body alive in the world, right now, and that is a better place to begin than most self-help advice would have you believe. Once you allow wonder to arrive, it proves to be astonishingly promiscuous. Over the past year, I’ve made a point of asking people about what inspires daily wonder. I expected themes to emerge, but I also assumed that those themes would be predictable: sunsets and forests; notable structures or works of art. Many people did describe the familiar icons of awe—the moment when an airplane breaks through the clouds, and all you can see is the great expanse of sky ahead; the experience of strangers gathering silently along a shoreline to watch the sun vanish below the horizon. But just as many have told me that they’ve found wonder in the mundane—watching a procession of ants carrying things they value, for instance. I’ve corresponded with people who described their affection for an old refrigerator; a sewing machine whose hums and clicks became a friend’s familiar voice; pots and pans that carried decades of family meals; and even a toilet whose owner began to admire it after a repair revealed its elegant method of operation. Whenever you cease treating the world as scenery or equipment, you’ll find that you encounter the world as something inexhaustibly, constantly present. Oceans and ants, sewing machines and sunsets, strangers and screen doors, microwaves and mountains, rocks and racquets, lamps and laughter all prove equally worthy. Our expectations for wonder sometimes wane, but it is with us all along, waiting to be rediscovered. This article appears in the October 2026 print edition with the headline “The Missing Ingredient of Modern Life.”
The Atlantic
Am I Talking to You, or ChatGPT?
2026-09-04

Am I Talking to You, or ChatGPT?

The Atlantic | 2026-09-04

Visit Original Website
Five years ago, people who hang out in the stranger corners of the web started arguing that the internet was mostly fake. It had been this way since about 2016, they said, when Google introduced its artificial neural network for machine translation. Supposedly, from that point forward, online interactions began to be replaced, until nearly every message and picture had been posted by a bot. Everywhere you looked, websites were “empty and devoid of people,” as one scholar of the theory put it. This wasn’t true, but the core idea was tethered to a real phenomenon. Even way back then, in 2021, social platforms were awash in fake views, fake likes, fake traffic, and fake engagement. Some people figured that automation might one day take over the entire web and then our world. They were mostly being silly, or paranoid. Today they seem like … prophets? “Dead Internet” theory is seeing a revival, this time in mainstream conversation. It’s no longer strange to think that AI might be everywhere, edging out all forms of human interaction: bots talking to bots, all the way down. Every week we seem to take another step in that direction. In June, the Pew Research Center reported that nearly a quarter of American adults now use chatbots every day. Two months later, OpenAI announced an official ChatGPT integration for iMessage. Chatbot-generated text is already widely used for website filler, business emails, and cover letters. Will it soon be commonplace in private messages between friends and family? Read: Maybe you missed it, but the internet ‘died’ in 2016 The ChatGPT-iMessage integration works on your desktop, not your phone. It doesn’t send messages on your behalf until you’ve reviewed and approved its suggestions. A similar plug-in has long been available for Anthropic’s Claude, and many messaging apps already offer AI-suggested replies. To some extent, OpenAI’s new product simply formalizes something that people had been doing on their own, either by pasting text from one window to another, or by improvising personal ChatGPT-linking software. (The Atlantic has a corporate partnership with OpenAI.) “Low-effort communication has always existed,” Michael Pfister, a software developer living in Colorado, told me. He said that he had already vibe coded his own version of the iMessage tool months ago. ChatGPT pings him daily to let him know if he’s missed any important questions in his group chat. He tried having it write a text to his friends, just as an experiment, but they immediately noticed that the message was AI. “They tried to prompt-inject me,” he said: They asked the agent what Michael thought of them, in the hopes of tricking it into sharing his private thoughts. He told me that he mostly uses the tool for work and that he isn’t interested in having AI communicate with his friends on his behalf in the future. “I think relationships are experiential,” he said. Max Weinbach, an analyst at a market-research firm in Connecticut, told me he uses ChatGPT to text with his bosses about work. “It just makes life easy,” he said. It’s hardly different from using Google’s suggested email responses, which have been available for nearly a decade. Weinbach had no ethical qualms about switching to LLM-assisted communication, although he recognizes that it can be taken as a sign of disrespect. “It’s not accepted or polite,” he said. “It’s just kind of a rude thing to do.” These two are on the bleeding edge, yet exhibiting more restraint than some others. All over Reddit, my favorite repository for human drama, people are worrying about human drama that has a nonhuman interloper. New posts appear each day requesting help in figuring out whether something might have been created with AI: Is this apartment listing real? What about this dating-app profile? What about this video of a cute animal doing something amazing? (A bobcat flagging down a biker to help her rescue her kitten from the bottom of a random hole?) Many of the requests have a moralistic tone, and the poster comes to the crowd solemnly, with hesitation, because they know the truth may be painful. The most fraught discussions concern AI-generated personal messages. Guys on dating apps are using the technology. Sometimes long-term partners, friends, siblings, or parents do it too. “Is this text apology written by ChatGPT?” someone will ask. Or “Did my mother use ChatGPT to write me a text of support on the morning of my divorce?” The implication is betrayal. I spoke with Melissa Crowder, a 47-year-old from Alberta, Canada, who shared her own experience with AI texting in a July post on Reddit. She’d spent all day having a “nice conversation” with a match from the dating app Feeld, then finally noticed that their repartee was off when he copy-pasted her own message back to her by mistake. At that point she realized all of his previous messages had been structurally similar—usually two paragraphs of text followed by a question on a single line. And he’d been using the word dangerously way too much. When she asked if he was using ChatGPT, he replied, “Busted” with a bunch of emoji, which also struck her as something ChatGPT might do. “I’m like, this is still ChatGPT.” Then he unmatched her. Crowder would not have been that mad if the guy had just fessed up and said something normal in his own voice. She sometimes uses AI tools to help her express herself too. She said she has a tendency to ramble on and overexplain her feelings, so she’ll use a site called Goblin Tools to self-edit. She might make a message to her ex-husband “Less emotional” (one of the choices in a drop-down menu) and get straight to the point. She also has a friend who sends AI-written texts from time to time. “Where I would draw my line is where you take the person out of the conversation,” she said. “If I’m vibing with a chatbot and then I meet the person in real life, what is that conversation going to look like?” For a recent paper, a group of researchers from the University of Washington and Arizona State University studied two popular Reddit forums—r/RealOrAI and r/isthisAI—to figure out how people go about determining which material is AI-generated and which is not. The most common mode of analysis evaluates the fundamental plausibility of an image or a video. (“Babies do not nearly have that much coordination to both be standing up, talking so well and holding up that large cat.”) A smaller number of comments in the study addressed some alleged telltale signs of AI-style texting, including the prevalence of “overly ornate but meaningless phrases.” Other people have noted LLMs’ overreliance on certain words (delve is an oft-noted one), sentence structures (negative parallelisms), and punctuation (em dashes). The paper ends in an ominous tone. “Our findings suggest that people are developing mental models of what AI-generated content looks like, built on personal experiences and beliefs developed over time,” the authors wrote, before arguing that these mental models were unlikely to be accurate for long. The LLMs will evolve and stop doing the things that now make their output so easy to spot. In fact, the tricks enumerated in the paper are already getting old, one of the paper’s authors, Tina Yeung, told me. “I definitely think this is a snapshot in time,” she said. The mere fact of people’s concern about AI, and their anxiety over its incursion into day-to-day life, may be a snapshot too. Perhaps the stigma that now exists around using ChatGPT to text a friend or message on a dating app will fade in the years ahead. Maybe one day it will disappear altogether, and even our phones will end up empty and devoid of people. Or else I guess it’s possible—just possible—that the Dead Internet theory is as silly as it ever was.
The Atlantic
Trump’s Antitrust Gambit May Backfire
2026-09-04

Trump’s Antitrust Gambit May Backfire

The Atlantic | 2026-09-04

Visit Original Website
In March, one of the most important antitrust cases in recent memory went to trial. The Department of Justice and 39 states plus the District of Columbia had sued the ticketing-and-entertainment giant Live Nation, alleging that its 2010 merger with Ticketmaster had created an effective monopoly on live shows. According to the suit, Live Nation controls 70 percent of what the government defined as “major concert venues” in the United States, and Ticketmaster handles 86 percent of concert ticketing at those venues. The DOJ accused the company of exploiting this monopoly by compelling venues to use Ticketmaster as their exclusive seller, jacking up prices, and saddling concertgoers with unavoidable junk fees. This certainly looked like an archetypal case of a company’s size leaving consumers worse off—precisely what our antitrust laws are designed to prevent. This is what made it so disappointing when, a week after the trial began, DOJ announced that it had reached a settlement with Live Nation. Although the agreement mandated changes to some of the company’s practices, including granting access to other booking agents at some venues and limiting fees, the deal fell well short of the more aggressive remedies that many people had hoped for, including—most obvious—breaking up the company. This odd turn of events makes sense only because we know now that the decision to settle was made not by anyone in DOJ’s Antitrust Division but by the president himself, according to a recent report in The Wall Street Journal. Just before the trial was set to begin, Donald Trump met with the CEO of Live Nation in the White House a couple of times and allegedly told a senior DOJ official, “Settle it.” In other words, the federal government abandoned one of its strongest antitrust lawsuits in years because Trump wanted it to. Quinta Jurecic: DOJ enters a new, even more aggressive phase This may then seem like yet another story about how Trump has wrecked norms and corrupted the rule of law. But in this case, there’s a twist. After the Department of Justice announced in March that it was settling with Live Nation, many observers expected that the states involved would drop the suit and sign on to the deal, as typically occurs when federal regulators reach a settlement. But in this case, 32 states—including Republican-led Florida, Tennessee, Texas, and Utah—and D.C. refused to settle. In April, they won their case: A jury found that Live Nation had illegally monopolized the live-events business and overcharged consumers. The judge is still determining the remedy, but it will almost certainly be tougher than the deal Live Nation got from DOJ. This will not be a one-off. A peculiarity of federal antitrust law is that state attorneys general can bring lawsuits on behalf of their citizens for violations of the Sherman Antitrust Act, and many states also have their own antitrust laws on the books. This gives them a great deal of leeway to act on their own. And because of the Trump administration’s attitude toward antitrust laws, what we saw in the Live Nation case—states refusing to defer to the feds, and instead pursuing litigation—will become more common. This is because Trump, despite having campaigned as a populist who would fight to lower prices, has presided over an administration that’s been almost completely deferential to the interests of businesses looking to get bigger. Since his return to office, the Department of Justice has filed exactly one lawsuit to block a merger—the $14 billion acquisition of Juniper Networks by the Hewlett Packard Enterprise Company—only to quickly reach a settlement. Stanley Woodward, a top DOJ official who oversees antitrust enforcement, appears to be an active opponent of antitrust litigation. Bloomberg reports that he’s wondered aloud in meetings why DOJ can’t simply stop reviewing deals. That’s not exactly the guy you want in charge of preventing monopolies. As the Live Nation case shows, even when a lawsuit does move forward, CEOs can simply go to Trump to get his support. After describing the settlement as “shocking” to lawmakers on Capitol Hill in May, Roger Alford, a Notre Dame law professor and former Antitrust Division senior official in Trump’s first term, observed that Trump’s interference in the case both undermined norms and set a new precedent for behind-the-scenes advocacy: “The rule of law quickly becomes the rule of lobbyists.” Read: Even when Ticketmaster works, it doesn’t Presidents historically avoided interfering directly with antitrust cases. The exceptions were few and notoriously corrupt (such as Richard Nixon’s meddling in an antitrust lawsuit against the International Telephone & Telegraph Corporation in exchange for a financial donation to the Republican National Convention). But Trump views the entire executive branch as an extension of his will, and he has turned regulatory agencies and DOJ into tools he can use to dole out favors, reward friends, and punish enemies. The only companies that have to worry about a federal antitrust prosecution at this point are those that Trump fiercely dislikes. But as the Live Nation case demonstrates, any sighs of relief among leaders of America’s oversize businesses would be premature. In an ideal world, antitrust regulations wouldn’t be fragmented and enforced by a panoply of states. But when the president effectively turns national antitrust laws into dead letters, the virtues of federalism become apparent: States will do what DOJ fails to do. This arrangement also makes it harder for companies to simply lobby their way to good deals across multiple states. The paradox is that by trying to hand out sweetheart arrangements to companies that are either trying to merge or flexing their monopoly power, Trump has made it more likely that those companies will have to navigate a gantlet of state lawsuits. And in asserting total control over the federal government, Trump has ended up making state governments much more aggressive and influential than they were before.

The Vicious Cycle Trump May Restart
2026-09-03

The Vicious Cycle Trump May Restart

The Atlantic | 2026-09-03

Visit Original Website
By the time he retired from the Marine Corps, in 1931, Major General Smedley Butler had served in Mexico, Haiti, Cuba, the Philippines, Nicaragua, the Dominican Republic, Honduras, and China. It was the golden age of American imperialism, and Butler was one of its golden boys, among the most decorated fighters in U.S. history. But over time, Butler began to have doubts about what he was doing. “I spent 33 years and 4 months in active service,” he wrote in 1935. “During that period I spent most of my time being a high-class muscle man for Big Business, for Wall Street and for the bankers. In short, I was a racketeer; a gangster for capitalism.” The costs of these excursions, he wrote, were paid in taxpayer money and soldiers’ lives. The wars that Butler fought also left scars around the world, as the journalist Jonathan M. Katz wrote in a 2022 biography. Little remembered in the United States, these interventions were formative for the populations they affected, fueling enduring anti-Americanism. The Trump administration seems determined to re-create the cycle in Venezuela today: military action, puppet government, exploitation of oil reserves, and almost certainly more resentment, fueling further conflict in the future. In January, U.S. troops descended on Venezuela and seized President Nicolás Maduro. Although President Trump offered federal drug-trafficking charges against Maduro as window dressing, he made no secret of the real reason for the incursion: He wanted to seize the country’s huge oil reserves. This week, the president claimed that he would refill the U.S. Strategic Petroleum Reserve with Venezuelan oil, “a Gift from Venezuela to the People of the United States.” The Trump administration has, as usual, offered few concrete details, but as my colleague Jonathan Chait writes, this is less a “deal” than “a payment by Venezuela of tribute at gunpoint.” The Venezuelan people are in no position to consent to handing over the country’s most valuable natural resource to a richer regional superpower for free, because Venezuela has not had fair elections in years. After deposing Maduro, the United States did not clear away his government or try to install the pro-democracy dissident (and Nobel Peace Prize laureate) María Corina Machado; in fact, it has created barriers to her return. Instead, it has enlisted a former Maduro lieutenant as president, while Secretary of State Marco Rubio oversees her as a sort of viceroy. Besides, nothing in international relations is as simple as a gift. The current arrangement involves Alejandro Betancourt López, a Venezuelan oil oligarch who has been under investigation in several countries. Betancourt managed to maintain connections with both Maduro’s regime and the U.S. government, and to dodge some charges thanks to help from Rudy Giuliani. Chevron is expanding its footprint in Venezuela, although other oil companies have been reluctant. Investors from other sectors, including crypto, are also getting involved. Although much money stands to be made, whether and how much ordinary Americans will actually benefit is less clear. Rebuilding Venezuelan production will take years, and the country’s oil is heavy and difficult to refine. That means no near-term relief at gas pumps and years before the U.S. reserve is refilled. Venezuelan oil and U.S. interests have a long and rocky history. American oil companies invested heavily in the country after World War II, but in 1976, the Venezuelan government nationalized the oil industry. In the early ’90s, the government once again opened to private companies. But Hugo Chávez, who portrayed himself as an anti-colonial hero and liked to use “Gringos go home!” as a slogan, became president in 1999. In 2007, he expropriated foreign assets once more, in what his energy minister called “an act of sovereignty for our country, for our people.” Trump cited the expropriation this year as another reason for U.S. intervention. Chávez’s approach, maintained under his protégé Maduro, failed. The oil industry, along with the rest of the national economy, has atrophied. In an article last year, my colleague Gisela Salim-Peyer eloquently recounted how Venezuela slid into authoritarianism under Chávez and Maduro. International observers described the 2018 presidential election as rigged, and evidence gathered by Machado convincingly showed that her party had won the 2025 race. A huge number of people fleeing Venezuela have flocked to the United States. For now, under the rump Chávez-Maduro regime, Venezuelans don’t have much say, but guessing how this might play out doesn’t require a great deal of creativity—just a working knowledge of history. Trump’s expropriation of Venezuelan oil won’t be well received by most people, nor will a Trump-driven gold rush by American businesses, no matter how much Venezuela needs assistance standing up its oil industry. Railing against the deal and foreign oil interests will be an easy populist stance for future Venezuelan politicians; perhaps one will even feel moved to nationalize assets once more—and so the cycle can begin again. You don’t have to be an anti-colonial peacenik to worry about the risks of Trump’s oil raid. You can just grasp that having hostile demagogues in power in the hemisphere is bad for U.S. interests, driving global instability as well as the kind of mass migration that Trump has described as a crisis. Such downsides help explain why anti-interventionism had become popular among Republicans over the past decade. Until the Venezuela raid, Trump portrayed himself as “America first,” but the foremost voice was J. D. Vance, who cited his experience deployed in Iraq in his speech accepting the GOP nomination for vice president. “As always, America’s ruling class wrote the checks. Communities like mine paid the price,” he said, sounding much like his fellow Marine Smedley Butler. “For decades, that divide between the few, with their power and comfort in Washington, and the rest of us only widened.” Yet now the Trump administration has embarked on just the sort of adventure Butler led and then decried. Wealthy Americans will benefit today, ordinary Americans will see little immediate reward, and the United States will face the blowback for decades to come. Here are three new stories from The Atlantic: Time-Travel Thursdays: Technology was once seen as countercultural, Jake Lundberg writes. He looks through The Atlantic’s archives to trace how that identity has evolved. The Netherlands Will Never Be Rid of Louisiana Crawfish Confronted with a seasoned, boiled, arguably delicious crayfish, some of the people of the Netherlands are not sure exactly what to do. “Do you suck the head out?” I heard from the table next to me earlier this summer, at Two Chefs, a restaurant in Amsterdam that hosts a monthly crayfish boil. Yes is the answer: The best way to eat a crayfish is to shell the tail, then slurp the flavored juices out of the head, and pick out the remaining meat with your hands. Reflect. The Met’s John Galliano exhibition had problems beyond the designer’s past transgressions, Kimberly Chrisman-Campbell argues. Explore. Faith Hill explores how “somatics” became the solution to modern life. The bluesman Son House’s classic “Death Letter” recounts the shock of hearing about a loved one’s death. I felt a little of that shock when I learned of the death of the singer Cassandra Wilson this week. She was just 70. As regular readers may know, I’m a jazz obsessive, but I don’t listen to many singers; jazz vocals too often come off as affected, cutesy, or outdated. Wilson wasn’t like that. She wasn’t like anybody, though she showed the influence of the great Abbey Lincoln. Wilson, a member of the influential collective M-Base, had real avant-garde credentials, but she also loved to adapt homelier material: Bob Dylan, Miles Davis, the Monkees, and old folk songs, all of it made personal and new. Her career was a forceful reminder that although the music we label “Americana” is heavily white and male, it couldn’t exist without Black women. To my ear, Wilson was also the greatest blues singer of our era. Her rendition of “Death Letter” is as haunting, visceral, and deep as any recording in the genre’s long history. Rafaela Jinich contributed to this newsletter. When you buy a book using a link in this newsletter, we receive a commission. Thank you for supporting The Atlantic.

How the Early Dreams of the Internet Changed
2026-09-03

How the Early Dreams of the Internet Changed

The Atlantic | 2026-09-03

Visit Original Website
This is an edition of Time-Travel Thursdays, a journey through The Atlantic’s archives to contextualize the present. Sign up here. During the 1984 Super Bowl, an advertisement launched a technological revolution. If you were alive, you probably know the one. Directed by Ridley Scott, the minute-long spot showed men in gray uniforms, heads shaved, eyes blank, marching in unison toward a lifeless auditorium. A Big Brother figure speaks from a giant screen, droning on about “the first glorious anniversary of the Information Purification Directives.” All the while, a woman in red shorts and a white tank top is running to the auditorium. She looks like she’s just come from an aerobics class, but she has a sledgehammer, and authorities are in pursuit. Entering the room, she hurls the sledgehammer into Big Brother’s face. There’s a flash of light, and a wind blows over the stunned, awakened audience. A different voice—a comforting one—announces, “On January 24th, Apple Computer will introduce Macintosh. And you’ll see why 1984 won’t be like 1984.” Although the ad did not show an actual Macintosh computer, it worked. Apple went on to sell 250,000 of its user-friendly, mouse-driven little machines in that year alone. The ad also asserted what was to be a defining idea of the emerging digital age: that personal computers, far from massive instruments of centralized control, were vessels of individual freedom. The coming of the internet, with its invitation to universally accessible knowledge and the free exchange of ideas, seemed to affirm the promise: Our machines wouldn’t enslave us; they would liberate us. The dystopia depicted in the ad came by way of George Orwell’s imagination, but the fears underlying it had deep American roots. As mass consumer culture expanded in the early 20th century, American artists, intellectuals, and critics worried over the problem of “conformity” and the loss of individualism that came with it. Most didn’t see the dark totalitarianism of 1984; instead, they saw a world of ready-made desires, a soulless existence brought to you by corporations, advertisers, media, and politicians. As The Atlantic’s review of Sinclair Lewis’s Babbitt put it in 1922, “the ‘standardized American’” was becoming “as much a national product as the Ford car”—a person like Babbitt himself, whose entire life and very personality were shaped by forces beyond him. Such criticism formed a fault line that ran from the cultural commentary of the ’20s to the outbursts of Beats, folkies, and academics in the ’50s. The earthquake, of course, would come in the counterculture of the 1960s. No longer would people need to be stuffed into “Little Boxes,” as the folk singer Malvina Reynolds worried early in the decade. The counterculture promised liberation from such confinement. Visiting San Francisco’s Haight-Ashbury district for The Atlantic in 1967, the novelist Mark Harris wondered whether he was seeing Babbitt’s descendants finally rebelling against conformity in ways that Babbitt himself could not. The counterculture’s sensibilities migrated into the mainstream, where popular culture and advertising absorbed its language of liberation and transgression, so that admen could pitch 7 Up using hippie iconography or Virginia Slims cigarettes by way of the women’s-liberation movement. Digital culture would prove especially receptive to these countercultural ideals. Northern California, after all, was the center of both the counterculture and the tech industry, and the cross-pollination between the two was considerable, as the historian Fred Turner has shown. By the 1990s, as the internet was becoming a mass medium, some of its first boosters (many of whom had roots in the counterculture) were presenting it as the realization of countercultural ideals. John Perry Barlow, a poet and sometimes Grateful Dead lyricist, wrote his 1996 “Declaration of the Independence of Cyberspace” in response to an early effort to regulate the internet. It reads like a Port Huron Statement for digital culture, describing a realm of individual freedom and free-flowing ideas that would break from existing forms of cultural and institutional authority. According to Barlow, a new “civilization of the Mind” was coming. In the dot-com and digital revolutions that followed, many of the people who sold the hardware, coded the software, and built the platforms were seen as iconoclasts themselves. Digital entrepreneurship became a form of transgression, not just in the casual dress and informal culture of the Silicon Valley office but also in what it promised to users. Tech visionaries pledged to create a world that would make possible precisely the kind of self-expression, open exchange, and communal connection that the counterculture had promised. It was no coincidence that by the late 1990s, Apple’s advertising—again with almost no reference to the machines themselves—simply pictured groundbreaking figures such as Gandhi, Martin Luther King Jr., Bob Dylan, and John Lennon, alongside the slogan “Think Different.” In many ways, those promises were fulfilled. The internet did splinter 20th-century mass culture, and the effects have been every bit as revolutionary as promised. We don’t all watch the same networks anymore; we don’t see the same movies; we don’t get our news from the same places or read the same books (mostly, we just don’t read); we “do our own research.” Even as the conditions of mass culture and conformity that shaped the counterculture and the early dreams of the internet have faded, we continue to live in their shadow. In the fractured age of social media and the unknowable algorithm, our devices give us the feeling of freedom, even as they serve the interests of corporate forces that track our movements, catalog our interests, and profile our wants. The oppressive monoculture has been replaced by countless minicultures, each with their own devoted acolytes; we still inhabit little boxes, partially of our own making. The transgressive ethos, meanwhile, continues to thrive. Subversion is perhaps the dominant language of digital culture, spoken across the political spectrum by podcasters “just asking questions,” wellness gurus swilling at-home health hacks, manosphere kingpins lashing out against wokeness, and lefty influencers denouncing the billionaire class. Everyone, it seems, is hurling a sledgehammer at something.

The Hasan Piker Question
2026-09-03

The Hasan Piker Question

The Atlantic | 2026-09-03

Visit Original Website
Like late-summer humidity, Hasan Piker hangs heavy over the discourse. At a rally on Monday, Vice President Vance brought up the hard-left streamer in an attempt to paint the Democratic Senate candidate Abdul El-Sayed as anti-American. Last week, House Minority Leader Hakeem Jeffries called Piker’s recent statements about Jewish Americans  “dangerous and anti-Semitic.” Senators Jon Ossoff, Cory Booker, and Jacky Rosen said much the same. Facing questions about a totally different set of Piker comments, Representative Alexandria Ocasio-Cortez hustled into an elevator. Some Democrats are trying to dismiss the 35-year-old political commentator as irrelevant. He’s just “a streamer in California,” El-Sayed said on Saturday, waving off questions from reporters. Piker himself has spent the past week casting himself as the victim of hysteria. “I have become this media cycle’s ‘trans athletes’ or ‘migrant caravan,’” he posted on X. But it was El-Sayed who first made Piker central to his campaign by bringing him out on the trail, partying poolside with him at an influencer event, and celebrating with him during his Election Night rally. In his thousands of hours of streaming, Piker has produced a dense catalog of comments spanning the spectrum from stupid to incredibly offensive, including several iterations of the phrase “I hate America” and, perhaps most memorably, that “America deserved 9/11.” (He quickly attempted to walk it back.) Piker also flaunts his authoritarian sympathies, calling Mao Zedong “one of the great leaders of this world” and counting the collapse of the Soviet Union among the “catastrophes” of the 20th century. Last week, he warned that “eventually someone is going to come around and take action” against American Jews who are “singularly invested in Israel.” Hasan Piker is part of the conversation now. He has become central to the Republican strategy in Michigan, where El-Sayed will continue to be asked about him. So, too, will Democrats across the country. The question ahead of November is whether their answers will satisfy voters. El-Sayed has moved away from Piker since winning the primary last month. The streamer has not been on the trail in Michigan. “I have spoken with the good doctor,” Representative Jim Clyburn told reporters of El-Sayed, who has a medical degree. “He has apologized. He says he will not campaign with Piker anymore.” After Piker’s comments last week about American Jews, El-Sayed said in a statement that anti-Semitism is a “scourge” and that “I stand against any rhetoric that puts the community at risk.” When asked about Piker at a meeting with a group of Jewish Democrats on Saturday, though, El-Sayed declined to disavow him. “If you want to make this race about a streamer in California, I just think you’re missing the boat.” Anyone could have predicted the Piker blowback. Plenty of people did. In March, one of El-Sayed’s primary opponents, Mallory McMorrow, compared Piker to the white-supremacist influencer Nick Fuentes, and leaders of the centrist think tank Third Way wrote a column urging Democrats not to associate with Piker. “Everyone saw it coming from a mile away, so they should’ve been better prepared to handle it,” one Democratic strategist told me. Even some of El-Sayed’s own backers felt similarly. “Not all socialism is toxic to the Democratic brand, but Piker certainly is,” one progressive strategist told me. (None of the Democrat-aligned operatives I reached out to would agree to speak on the record for this story, citing their support for El-Sayed as well as their fear of reprisal from Piker’s rabid online fans.) The Piker drama is a ribbon-wrapped gift to Republicans in a year when so much else—inflation, high gas prices, an unpopular president—is working against them. They seem well aware of this. Mike Rogers, El-Sayed’s Republican opponent, refers to Piker as El-Sayed’s “running mate.” “When I see Abdul El-Sayed campaign around this state with somebody who said that America deserved 9/11, what I see is a man who has sympathy and compassion for people who share his religion, but has nothing for people who don’t,” Vance said at the rally on Monday. “What I’d ask you to remember is that whoever you send to Washington, whatever policy views you have or you don’t have, send people of good heart who focus on the right issues, who fight for the right people, and who love the United States of America.” The strategy is obvious: Identify every repellent thing Piker has ever said, tie Piker to El-Sayed, and advertise the relationship at every opportunity. There is more than a hint of irony in this situation. President Trump has shown a strong affinity for shock jocks and trolls. In 2020, he awarded Rush Limbaugh, who had a recurring segment celebrating the deaths of gay people from AIDS, the Presidential Medal of Freedom. Laura Loomer, a far-right activist and an ally of Trump’s, last week referred to a Senate candidate as a “ghetto black communist bitch.” Some Democrats, seeing Trump suffer little political consequence for such associations, believe it’s unfair that El-Sayed should have to answer for Piker’s actions. But politics, like life, is famously unfair. Democrats are constantly being asked to condemn Piker in cable-news hits and hallway interviews, even outside Michigan. Several Jewish Democrats have weighed in too. Through a spokesperson, Ossoff said he “unreservedly rejects reckless and dangerous rhetoric suggesting that policy advocacy invites violence against the Jewish community or any community.” Representative Josh Gottheimer of New Jersey said that “anyone who campaigns with Piker or elevates him is complicit in the rising antisemitism and anti-American hatred in this country.” Moderate Democrats are seizing the moment as a chance to engage in a little centrist posturing. Senator John Fetterman went on Fox News and delivered a challenge to El-Sayed: “I’m offering $50,000 for you to do a rally with Hasan Piker” on September 11, he said. Piker was not immediately available for comment for this story. But last week, after several Jewish Democrats criticized him, he dismissed their concerns by implying they reflected an orchestrated conspiracy against him, writing on X that an “aipac boss call was made.” Maybe El-Sayed could quell some of the frenzy himself. In 2008, controversial comments from Barack Obama’s former pastor Jeremiah Wright threatened to upend Obama’s bid for the White House. In a sermon shortly after September 11, the pastor said that “America’s chickens are coming home to roost.” Later, Wright said, “No, no, no, not God Bless America. God damn America—that’s in the Bible—for killing innocent people.” Obama chose to address the controversy directly, and delivered a speech condemning Wright’s remarks while offering context about America’s racial tensions. That speech “was a masterpiece of empathy, and at the time it felt that it changed the trajectory of his campaign,” Michelle Goldberg wrote this week. At the very least, some Democrats argue, El-Sayed could more plainly denounce his friend. “Say, ‘Here’s what I condemn; here’s what I disavow. I disagree; it was wrong and hurtful,’” the progressive strategist said. “If Piker says something new that’s bad—which, if I was a betting man, I’d say will happen—you come out against it swiftly.” Michigan, a state that Trump won in 2016 and 2024, was never going to be an easy win for El-Sayed. His relationship with Piker, who is paid to provoke, not persuade, isn’t going to make that task any simpler. The good news for Democrats is that most people don’t actually know Piker; polling from earlier this year showed that 60 percent of likely voters have never heard of him. The bad news for Democrats is that Republicans will spend the next two months working diligently to change that.

Trump’s Attack on the Postal Service Echoes Another President’s Shameful Plot
2026-09-04

Trump’s Attack on the Postal Service Echoes Another President’s Shameful Plot

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-04

Visit Original Website
Sign up for Executive Dysfunction, a weekly newsletter that highlights one under-the-radar story about how Trump is changing the law—or how the law is pushing back—and keeps you posted on the latest from Slate’s Jurisprudence team. President Donald Trump’s executive order directing the post office to suppress mail voting has often been called “unprecedented.” That is not quite right. An earlier president—in fact, one of Trump’s role models—also empowered the Postal Service to squelch political opposition. It was one of the most disreputable episodes in U.S. history, and Trump has attempted to revive it with a modern twist. In the summer of 1835, the American Anti-Slavery Society began mailing a pamphlet titled Human Rights, along with other literature, to thousands of civic and religious leaders in Southern states, hoping to convince readers of the immorality of slavery. Things did not go as planned. That July, a shipment that included Human Rights, the Emancipator, and a children’s magazine called the Slave’s Friend arrived in Charleston, South Carolina. The local postmaster, a Jacksonian Democrat named Alfred Huger, was unnerved by what he considered inflammatory content and sequestered the abolitionist material, rather than comply with his legal obligation to deliver the mail. That evening, a mob of so-called lynch men broke into the warehouse and removed the mailbags, which they soon consigned to a great bonfire that attracted thousands of cheering spectators. Huger wrote for instructions to Postmaster General Amos Kendall, as well as New York Postmaster Samuel Gouverneur, from whose office the offending literature had been shipped. Gouverneur agreed to refuse acceptance of abolitionist shipments pending a ruling from Kendall. Kendall had been a key backer of Democrat Andrew Jackson in the 1828 election, having defected from Henry Clay’s Whigs. His reward was appointment to Jackson’s “Kitchen Cabinet,” in which he was widely considered one of the president’s most loyal advisers. After his reelection in 1832, Jackson nominated Kendall to be postmaster general. The appointment was controversial, as Jackson’s opponents feared the consequences of putting such a partisan figure in charge of the nation’s communications. Nonetheless, Kendall was confirmed. He did not let Jackson down. Kendall’s reply to the Charleston postmaster was careful but unmistakable. Acknowledging that he lacked statutory authority to ban material from the mail, he nonetheless ruled against delivery of the abolitionist periodicals. “By no act, or direction of mine, official or private, could I be induced to aid, knowingly, in giving circulation to papers of this description, directly or indirectly,” he explained. Jackson, a slaveholder and the brutal enforcer of Indian removal, enthusiastically agreed that the “wicked plan of exciting the negroes to insurrection and to massacre” had to be stopped. He instructed Southern postmasters to record and publicize the names of even individual subscribers to abolitionist newspapers to encourage intimidation by their neighbors and perhaps even compel them to leave the South. In his message to Congress that year, Jackson recommended legislation to prohibit, “under severe penalties, the circulation in the Southern States, through the mail, of incendiary publications intended to instigate the slaves to insurrection,” which of course meant any abolitionist pamphlets or newspapers. Jackson’s proposal died in the Senate, but that did not end postal censorship of abolitionist periodicals. Many Southern states passed their own laws and resolutions mandating rejection of abolitionist material, on the spurious claim that it tended “to excite the slaves of the southern states to insurrection and revolt.” Southern postmasters thus continued to withhold antislavery periodicals, with the tacit approval of Jackson protégé Kendall. The local suppression of abolitionist mail persisted throughout the antebellum era during the administrations of Jackson’s eight successors, none of whom enforced the federal statute prohibiting detention of the mail. In 1857, for example, Attorney General Caleb Cushing, appointed by President James Buchanan, opined that Mississippi law superseded the federal postal statute because of the state’s right, under the Constitution, to protect itself “against ‘domestic violence’ and to make provisions to ‘suppress insurrection.’ ” Consequently, the postmaster in Yazoo City, Mississippi, could not be required to deliver printed matter that tended “to produce disaffection, disorder and rebellion among the colored population of the state.” Both Jackson’s and Trump’s efforts to manipulate the mail have rested on fabricated premises. In Jackson’s case, it was the racist myth that enslaved people were content with their condition and would remain passive unless instigated by Northern abolitionists. In Trump’s case, it is the myth that mail ballots—the same method Trump uses to vote—are a form of election rigging. Or, as he put it with characteristic mendacity and capitalization, “ELECTIONS CAN NEVER BE HONEST WITH MAIL IN BALLOTS.” The slavocrats’ abuse of the Postal Service ended only with the Civil War. The Supreme Court has the power to terminate Trump’s voter-suppression scheme much sooner. We shall see. Slate relies on advertising to support our journalism. The Wild Way Out of the Iran War That Still Manages to Be Better Than Any Alternative
2026-09-04

The Wild Way Out of the Iran War That Still Manages to Be Better Than Any Alternative

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-04

Visit Original Website
Sign up for the Slatest to get the most insightful analysis, criticism, and advice out there, delivered to your inbox daily. President Donald Trump is reportedly having private discussions with senior aides about whether he should end his war with Iran. The story, in the Wall Street Journal, rouses memories of Vermont Sen. George Aiken’s suggestion, early in the Vietnam War, that we should just “declare victory and go home.” Aiken didn’t really say quite that, but it would have been good if President Lyndon Johnson had taken up the idea then. And it would be very good if Trump followed his instincts—at least this instinct—now. He could say, as he has said many times, that he’s “obliterated” Iran’s nuclear program, that spaceborne cameras are constantly watching the area where its enriched uranium is buried deep inside mountains, and that if the Iranians try to extract it, we’ll bomb the site again. He could also recite statistics on the percentage of Iran’s missiles, ships, airplanes, etc., we’ve destroyed in the six-month war. These pronouncements would be either misleading or irrelevant. For example, the claim about Iran’s nuclear facilities has been true since his “Midnight Hammer” bombing raid in June 2025, well before the start of this war. The claim about decimating Iran’s military forces neglects the fact that they’re still able to stop traffic in the Strait of Hormuz and inflict damage on U.S. bases throughout the Middle East, which is why the war carries on. But Trump’s supporters—the only audience he cares about pleasing—will overlook these awkward facts and cheer his declaration of “Mission accomplished.” The rest of us will view these claims as another set of lies—but we will also take some comfort that at least the war is over. The fact is, the more Trump prolongs the war, the weaker the United States seems. It is pathetic enough that our air and naval forces—by most measures the mightiest in the world—have hit more than 15,000 targets in Iran, yet failed to accomplish any strategic goal. In fact, it did worse than nothing. The barrage, which among other things killed the top echelon of Iran’s political and spiritual leaders, only elevated the senior officers of Iran’s elite military unit, which is much more hard-line and oppressive—and is now in complete control of the country. Another unforeseen effect of the barrage was to show that America’s military wasn’t as strong as it seemed. In the course of hitting those 15,000 targets, the U.S. Air Force and Navy depleted a stunningly high percentage of the country’s most vital munitions—including more than half of its cruise missiles and high-tech air-defense weapons. The campaign also required replacing the USS Abraham Lincoln, one of the two aircraft carriers mobilized for the war, with the USS George Washington, which had been the only aircraft carrier the Navy had in the Pacific Fleet. (The Lincoln had to rotate out because early in the war, Iranian missiles ravaged the Navy’s supply hub in Bahrain, leaving the crew of 5,000 sailors bereft of fresh food and basic supplies.) The whole world is watching. What they’re seeing is pretty grim—a onetime superpower that no longer evokes much respect or fear. There’s no sign that appearances will soon brighten. In Iran, U.S. military pressure isn’t working, while economic pressure has its limits, as long as the Chinese keep helping Iran (as they’re likely to do, it being in their interest to keep the United States bottled up in the Middle East, far away from the Taiwan Strait and the South China Sea). Trump doesn’t know what to do. Nor does anyone around him. They are so desperate for ideas that a month ago, a senior intelligence officer in U.S. Central Command, which plans and executes military operations in the Middle East, emailed a broad group of analysts and officers, asking for “new creative and unconventional ways to pressure and punish Iran.” They—and thus we—are in a trap. The more Trump threatens to destroy all of Iran if they don’t do what he says (as he did again for the umpteenth time this week), and then backs off (which is better than following through but doesn’t do much for our image), the less credible any of our threats or promises seem. It is no surprise, then, that allies are looking elsewhere for supply chains and security—in part out of fear that our common adversaries may be tempted to take aggressive action in this vulnerable moment for America. We would all be better off if Trump just ended the war. Stop attacking Iran (and thus provoking Iran to attack us). Let Iran and Oman work out an arrangement to open the Strait of Hormuz; it would probably entail tolls, but that’s better than the uncertainties and grave costs of the current situation. Meanwhile, keep an eye on the potential revival of Iran’s nuclear program—which, for the moment, even the CIA concludes, poses no imminent threat. It would be better still if Trump then sent the USS George Washington back to the Pacific, fired Secretary of Defense Pete Hegseth for giving him lousy advice (it wouldn’t be the first time Trump turned a loyal aide into a scapegoat), and made polite phone calls to all of our allies (whom he’s lambasted for not helping him fight Iran), assuring them that he would consult with them before starting any wars in the future. This, of course, is fantasy (except maybe the part about sending the Washington back to its port in Japan). It’s certainly what any normal president would do—more to the point, it’s what any normal president would have done all along. That only amplifies how far Donald Trump is from normal. Trump’s Latest Attack on American Education Is Particularly Nefarious
2026-09-03

Trump’s Latest Attack on American Education Is Particularly Nefarious

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-03

Visit Original Website
This story is adapted from the Slatest newsletter. You can sign up here to get it in your inbox every day. Since Donald Trump returned to office, his administration has waged a war on supposed “wokeness” in education. On Thursday, it proposed new federal regulations that would target schools that explicitly aim to help nonwhite students. Here we go again. What exactly does this new rule say? Schools already have to meet certain requirements to qualify for 501(c)(3) tax-exempt status with the IRS. The new rule, which has yet to be finalized but is set to take effect in May, would add another. It states that any private school, trade school, or university that uses “race, color, or national or ethnic origin” in “admissions, educational policies, scholarships and loans, athletics, and every other school-administered or school-supported program” would no longer qualify for tax-exempt status. Many institutions have adopted policies to aid minority and underprivileged students in an attempt to balance out legacies of discrimination in both higher education and American society. But the Trump administration has cast those efforts as discriminatory against the students’ white and Asian peers. OK, but what would this rule mean in practical terms? Enforcing these guidelines would probably require IRS audits to determine whether a school’s policies or programs are in compliance. But the rule would seem to be worded broadly enough that a school that in any way supports, say, a club for Black students could conceivably be at risk. The IRS estimates that the new rule could strip scholarships from about 750,000 students and threaten the tax-exempt status of up to 18,000 schools nationwide, from private elementary schools to colleges. I get why that would be bad for students. But why is losing tax exemption bad for schools? Tax exemption, which private schools receive because education has long been considered a public good, saves some schools millions of dollars a year. It also lets people who give money to them deduct the donations from their taxes, an incentive that helps schools fundraise. Losing it could exacerbate financial struggles that have already caused some colleges to cut programs, lay off staff, and even close. Hang on: I thought the Supreme Court already outlawed affirmative action. So why is this even necessary? The new rule is part of an ongoing struggle to interpret the court’s landmark 2023 ruling, which banned race-conscious college admissions policies. In response, some schools have cut back on programs aimed at helping minority students, even relatively benign initiatives like special orientation events, and Black and Latino enrollment at top universities has fallen. But other schools have looked for wiggle room, and the new IRS rule does have some exceptions. It lets institutions continue to support underprivileged students as long as those efforts use “race-neutral criteria such as family income, geographic location, first-generation status, individual hardship, military family status, or academic achievement.” Even so, as schools try to walk Trump’s line under the new rule, they risk huge consequences if they err in a way the administration considers excessively “woke.” According to the New York Times, the administration already expects a suit from the American Association of University Professors, a group that supports academic freedom and has successfully challenged other Trump efforts to target higher education. And if the IRS does start auditing individual schools once the rule takes effect, the schools could contest attempts to take away their tax-exempt status in court. Threatening an organization’s tax-exempt status seems like an effort to intimidate it into complying in advance. Yes. Even the prospect of being investigated can lead organizations to steer clear of anything that might put them in Trump’s sights. In fact, that’s already happening. In previous cases, some colleges have settled with the administration (or are considering doing so) to end federal probes, or they’ve preemptively changed their policies to avoid scrutiny. Sometimes, controversy can be a tax in its own right. Now let’s pivot from higher education to higher purpose—like how to have a great rest of your evening. My colleagues think the following might help: A tribute to a feminist icon: Slate executive editor Susan Matthews (who also hosted Slow Burn: Roe v. Wade) remembers Gloria Steinem, who died on Wednesday at 92. Susan shares what she learned from Steinem’s long and successful career fighting for women’s rights. A movie so bad it’s good: Niu Lai, a poorly made Chinese film about animated cows, has quickly become one of the most profitable movies of all time. This feat is all the more impressive when you consider that “watching it is like mainlining a Pixar movie while severely concussed,” writes Franklin Schneider, who investigates why this amateur flick has been such a hit with audiences. Some dating advice for the modern era: Money is often a touchy subject in relationships, but especially when one person earns more than their partner. In the latest installment of Unhinged, Slate’s dating advice column, Steffi Cao counsels a woman whose dates are uncomfortable with her having the financial upper hand. Some wordplay to end the day: Take a break with today’s Pears, which has 10 rounds. Can you find the longest word? OK, it’s high time for me to leave you be for the night. See you tomorrow!
